A fresh look at the Peierls-Onsager substitution.
Horia D. Cornean, Bernard Helffer, Radu Purice

Abstract
We formulate a general version of the Peierls-Onsager substitution for a finite family of Bloch eigenvalues under a local spectral gap hypothesis, via strongly localized tight-frames and magnetic matrices. This extends the existing results to long-range magnetic fields without any slow-variation hypothesis and without any triviality assumption for the associated Bloch sub-bundle. Moreover, our results cover a large class of periodic, elliptic pseudo-differential operators. We also prove the existence of an approximate time evolution for initial states supported inside the range of the isolated Bloch family, with a precise error control.



1
Introduction

The phenomenology of solids in the presence of external electro-magnetic fields is an extremely rich field for both theoretical and applied research. The basic model for these phenomena considers charged quantum particles moving in a potential that is periodic with respect to a regular lattice
\Gamma\cong\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, describing the crystalline structure of the solid, under the action of some external electric and magnetic fields. An important feature implied by the periodicity of the potential is
the Bloch-Floquet decomposition
of the self-adjoint operator
H_{0}
that describes a quantum particle moving in such a potential in a
d
-dimensional affine space (see
[
17
]
and Appendix
A
). This means that
H_{0}
may be decomposed as a smooth family of self-adjoint operators
\{\widehat{H}(\xi)\}_{\xi\in\mathbb{T}_{*}}
(which we call fibre operators) acting on square integrable functions on the
d
-dimensional torus (
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
with
\mathbb{T}:=\mathbb{R}^{d}/\mathbb{Z}^{d}
) and indexed by
\xi\in\mathbb{T}_{*}
,
the dual torus
(see (
2.4
) and the following text in Subsection
2.2
). Moreover, as described in
[
17
]
, each fibre operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
has a compact resolvent and thus an unbounded discrete spectrum
\big\{\lambda_{k}(\xi)\,,\,k\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\},\,\xi\in\mathbb{T}_{*}\big\}
. The functions
\lambda_{k}
, called the
Bloch levels
, are continuous functions on the dual torus whose graphs might cross among themselves.
We identify functions on a
d
-dimensional torus with periodic functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

1.1
The Peierls-Onsager approximation procedure

In many situations there exists a group of Bloch levels which, although possibly crossing among themselves, stay well separated from the remaining infinite family of Bloch levels. Using the terminology introduced in
[
32
]
, we say that we have in this case an
isolated Bloch family
and a
local spectral gap condition
. We call it a
strictly isolated Bloch family
when its range is separated from the rest of the spectrum by some spectral gaps of the full operator
H_{0}
; in this case we say that the
global spectral gap condition
holds. In general, when such an isolated Bloch family
\mathfrak{B}
exists, one can define an infinite dimensional orthogonal projection
P_{\mathfrak{B}}
, invariant under translations with vectors from
\Gamma
and commuting with the Hamiltonian
H_{0}
such that under certain topological conditions on the structure of
P_{\mathfrak{B}}
, the dynamics of the states in
P_{\mathfrak{B}}L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is exactly given by the ’quantization’ of a matrix-valued function
\widehat{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\big(\widehat{H}(\theta)\big)
on the dual torus (see (
3.28
)) considered as a periodic function on the dual space
\widehat{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\xi)
. If the Bloch family is strictly isolated, then the projection
P_{\mathfrak{B}}
is a spectral projection of the operator
H_{0}
.

Let us mention here that some interesting physical consequences may be deduced when the so-called Fermi level of the given material is situated in the domain of such an isolated Bloch family, but we shall not consider these aspects in the present paper.

If the external magnetic field
B=dA
is weak, one of the widely used approximations in the physics literature is the celebrated Peierls-Onsager substitution. It states that an isolated Bloch family will continue to manifest itself in some given
spectral windows
of the operator
H^{A}
(perturbation of
H_{0}
by the magnetic field), through the existence of an ’approximatly’ invariant subspace on which the dynamics may be approximated by ”the minimal coupling procedure” applied to the matrix-valued function
\widehat{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\xi)
, i.e. the quantization of the matrix-valued function
\widehat{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\big(\xi-A(x)\big)
. Let us notice that that when the isolated Bloch family has only one element
\lambda_{k_{0}}(\xi)
and the topological condition is satisfied, then the matrix
\widehat{\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{B}}}
has dimension 1 with the unique element
\lambda_{k_{0}}(\xi)
, so that the approximate dynamics is given by the quantization of the periodic function
\lambda_{k_{0}}\big(\xi-A(x)\big)
.

It turns out that
transforming the above heuristic arguments into mathematical theorems is a rather challenging problem, the mathematics behind this approximating procedure being rather involved and thus a very large amount of literature has been devoted to it (let us cite just a few papers more closely related to our work
[
5
,
8
,
19
,
28
,
33
,
32
,
35
,
44
,
36
,
56
,
57
,
59
,
63
,
65
]
). We shall very briefly recall the main results of the existing literature in order to put into perspective our new results.

1.2
Very brief report on the existing results

We emphasize from the begining that the quasi-totality of the existing references on the subject considers the case with

H_{0}=-(2m)^{-1}\Delta+\mu{W}(x)\,,

(1.1)

with
\Delta
the Laplace operator on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
W
some real potential function,
m>0
the mass of the particles and
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
some coupling constant, with the magnetic perturbation given by:

H^{A}=-(2m)^{-1}\Delta^{A}+\mu{W}(x)\,,

(1.2)

with
the magnetic Laplacian
:

\Delta^{A}\,:=\,\underset{1\leq j\leq d}{\sum}\big(\partial_{j}-iA_{j}(x)\big)^{2}.

(1.3)

The main technical difficulty in dealing with magnetic perturbations comes when we consider magnetic fields that do not vanish fast enough towards infinity, having vector potentials whose derivatives might grow at infinity. In this case, the functions
\lambda\big(\xi-A(x)\big)
might no longer be Hörmander symbols.

A second, more subtle technical difficulty has a topological nature and is related with the vector bundle structure of the Bloch-Floquet decomposition that we briefly review in Appendix
A
. More precisely, the Bloch-Floquet decomposition of the orthogonal projection associated with the isolated Bloch family (that we shall precisely define in Subsection
2.4
) may allow for a smooth family of orthonormal basis, or not. In physical terms this smooth basis are known as localized composite Wannier functions (see also our Appendix
A
).

The first approaches to obtain a rigorous Peierls-Onsager approximation theory (see
[
5
,
35
,
36
,
65
,
33
]
) have considered
isolated Bloch families with only one Bloch level and constant magnetic fields
under the following two main assumptions:

Hypothesis 1.1
.

1.

the global gap condition;

2.

existence of a system of localized composite Wannier functions.

The constancy of the magnetic field has a very important technical consequence, namely the existence of
a projective representation of the translations with elements from

\Gamma
that commutes with the perturbed operator. They are the Zak translations, that we recall in Paragraph
6.1.1
. Extending these type of arguments to non-constant magnetic fields has unfortunately not been possible. The effort to relax the two assumptions in Hypothesis
1.1
and the constancy hypothesis on the magnetic field has generated a large number of improvements of these first results.

Starting with the paper
[
63
]
a new point of view has become dominant, namely to replace constancy of the magnetic field with
a slow variation hypothesis
, i.e. to consider regular magnetic fields (i.e. with components of class
BC^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
) controlled by a small parameter
\epsilon\in[0,\epsilon_{0}]
in the form:
B_{\epsilon}:=dA_{\epsilon}
with
A_{\epsilon}(x):=A(\epsilon{x})
for some
A
with smooth components and to use a variant of adiabatic procedure called
space-adiabatic perturbation theory
(SAPT)
[
62
,
66
]
. This procedure allowed for the elimination of the global gap hypothesis for an isolated Bloch family with any finite number of Bloch levels. Nevertheless, let us clearly emphasize here that the slow variation hypothesis is a strong limitation, defining a rather small and very special subclass of long-range regular magnetic fields: namely those fields having weak components of some order
\epsilon>0
with
k
-derivatives of order
\epsilon^{k}
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
; thus, on any finite region, for
\epsilon>0
small, the magnetic field has a very simple structure.

The second assumption in Hypothesis
1.1
remained necessary for practically all the following developments, except for the results in
[
32
]
where working with some non-trivial connections on the Bloch bundle allows the authors to treat some special situations when the localized composite Wannier functions do not exist. Nevertheless, one has to impose that the magnetic field is a slowly varying perturbation of a constant magnetic field, the non-constant part coming from a bounded magnetic potential.

Finally, let us mention the paper
[
19
]
where the authors propose the use of the magnetic pseudo-differential calculus (see
[
52
]
,
[
41
]
-
[
43
]
) in order to obtain a natural and efficient framework to deal with the Peierls-Onsager approximation and under Hypothesis
1.1
obtain an extension of the rigorous Peierls-Onsager approximation for strictly isolated Bloch families of a large class of magnetic pseudo-differential operators with elliptic symbols of strictly positive order and regular magnetic fields (i.e. with smooth components, bounded together with all their derivatives).

Let us also recall the approach using a kind of Grushin problem (
[
33
,
44
]
) that allowed for important extensions concerning the location of the perturbed spectrum close to the isolated Bloch family, without any information related to the approximate dynamics.

Thus, to our knowledge, in the current state of the art, the Peierls-Onsager substitution without a global gap considiton has been rigorously defined and controlled only for slowly varying long range perturbing magnetic fields and, left aside some very special cases (treated in
[
32
]
), only in the presence of localized composite Wannier functions.

1.3
Significance of our results

In this paper we use an abstract general theorem concerning finite rank vector bundles over finite dimensional compact manifolds in order to construct a Parseval frame (see Appendix
C
) associated with any finite isolated Bloch family, and use this frame and the magnetic pseudo-differential calculus in order to define and control a Peierls-Onsager approximation for regular long-range perturbing magnetic fields, without the global gap condition and without the hypothesis of existence of localized composite Wannier functions. Let us clearly state here what we mean by regular magnetic field;

Definition 1.2
.

We say that a magnetic field on a
d
-dimensional configuration space is
regular
, when its components are smooth and bounded together with their derivatives of all orders.

Our arguments apply to a large class of pseudo-differential operators with elliptic periodic symbols of strictly positive order (see Definition
2.7
), the case in (
1.1
) being viewed as associated with the elliptic symbol of second order:
(x,\xi)\mapsto(2m)^{-1}|\xi|^{2}+\mu{W}(x)
. In fact, as also stated in Definition
2.7
, our quantum Hamiltonians may also incorporate a
\Gamma
-periodic regular magnetic field satisfying Hypothesis
2.5
and thus having a
\Gamma
-periodic vector potential.

Finally, let us mention that we can make a more refined analysis for the case when the magnetic field perturbation has a non-zero constant part, i.e. with the following structure:

B^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}(x)=\epsilon\,\big(B_{0}+\mathcal{c}B_{1}(x)\big),

where
\mathcal{c}\in[0,1)
,
B_{0}
has constant components and
B_{1}(x)
is a long-range (non vanishing at infinity), regular magnetic field,
that is not supposed to be either periodic or slowly variable
. To this magnetic field one can always construct (see Appendix
B
) a magnetic vector potential
A^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}(x)
which is an
1
-form whose coefficients are smooth and grow at most linearly at infinity. This linear growth at infinity and the lack of slow variation are the main reasons for which the spatial adiabatic methods do not work here.

Moreover, an argument based on a Schur-Feshbach decomposition allows to compare the real dynamics and the approximate one with an error of order
\epsilon^{2}
for the spectrum, and of order
\epsilon
for the time evolution of states with energies in an associated spectral window.

Compared with the results of
[
32
]
, our approach allows non-constant magnetic field perturbations which do not come from bounded and slowly variable magnetic vector potentials and our effective operator is identified with a magnetic matrix instead of a pseudodifferential operator on a twisted two dimensional torus, and we may work in any dimension
d\geq 2
.

Regarding the construction of our Parseval frame, compared with
[
21
]
, what we do here is valid in any dimension
d\geq 2
. Also, the way in which we construct the magnetic frame from the non-magnetic one is completely different and it may be of independent interest. Finally, let us emphasize the elaboration in Subsection
4.3
of an abstract procedure replacing the Gram-Schmidt method and allowing to transform a frame into a Parseval frame.

1.4
Brief overview of the paper

The notations and conventions we use, as well as a number of basic facts from the literature are gathered in Subsection
2.2
.

Let us emphasize from the beginning that the entire formulation of our arguments is done in the framework of the magnetic pseudo-differential calculus (see
[
41
,
42
,
43
,
53
,
13
,
16
]
), which to our knowledge is the most natural one allowing for a precise mathematical formulation of the Peierls-Onsager substitution, as quantization of the matrix-valued
\Gamma_{*}
-periodic function
\widehat{\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{B}}}\big(\xi-A(x)\big)
as mentioned in Subsection
1.1
, for long-range regular magnetic fields.

Our approach to the Peierls-Onsager approximation is rather different from the one using the Space-Adiabatic Perturbation Theory, being a quite natural extension of the first approaches in
[
56
,
57
,
35
]
, based on three important technical new aspects:

•

the Parseval frames that one can build in the spaces of sections of finite rank vector bundles over finite dimensional compact manifolds, allowing to replace the lack of localized smooth Wannier functions,

•

the magnetic pseudo-differential calculus, allowing to deal with non-constant long-range regular magnetic fields,

•

a procedure based on the Schur complement used to obtain good spectral results for operators “reduced” to some “quasi-invariant” subspaces.

In Section
2
we present the main framework of our work, giving a precise formulation of the problem we consider and the main results we obtain.

Section
3
introduces the main technical construction needed for our strategy starting from a general abstract result in the theory of smooth Euclidean vector bundles and defining a Parseval frame
\text{\large{$\boldsymbol{\Psi}$}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
(
3.23
) for the closed subspace defined by the isolated Bloch family
\mathfrak{B}
in Hypothesis
2.10
.

Section
4
starts from some ideas in
[
67
,
49
]
of combining
\Gamma
-translations with a local change of gauge, generalizing a procedure used in
[
57
,
35
]
based on the Zak translations in constant magnetic field (see Definition
6.1
) in order to build a “perturbed Parseval frame”
\text{\large{$\boldsymbol{\Psi}$}}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}
in (
4.23
). The closed subspace generated by this frame is the image of the perturbed orthogonal projection
P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}
announced in point
1
of Theorem
2.18
. The different properties of
P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}
are proved using Remark
4.19
, the results in Subsection
3.4
, Proposition
3.12
, Remark
4.12
and the results of Paragraph
4.2.4
.

Section
5
uses the results in
[
2
,
24
]
concerning the continuous variation of the spectrum of the Hamiltonian with the intensity of weak regular magnetic fields and a variant of the Feshbach-Schur argument elaborated by the authors in
[
14
,
15
]
in order to obtain a relation between the resolvents of the
complete and the reduced perturbed Hamiltonians for a spectral parameter belonging to a certain “window”. In this way we prove Theorems
5.1
and
5.7
, which leads to the results stated in point
2
of Theorem
2.18
.

Concerning the results stated as point
3
of Theorem
2.18
, they follow from the conclusions and arguments in Subsections
4.2
-
4.4
using the perturbed Parseval frame
\text{\large{$\boldsymbol{\Psi}$}}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}
in (
4.23
). The proclaimed
C^{*}
-algebra injective morphism
\mathfrak{M}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is just the composition of the
C^{*}
-algebra morphism
\mathfrak{W}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}
defined in Remark
4.18
composed to the right with the natural isomorphism
\mathbb{B}\big(\ell^{2}(\Gamma)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}\big)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathscr{M}^{\circ}_{\Gamma}[\mathscr{M}(n_{\mathfrak{B}})]
induced by the canonical basis
\big\{{{\cal{e}}_{\alpha}\otimes{\cal{e}}_{p}}\big\}_{(\alpha,p)\in\Gamma\times\{1,\dots,n_{\mathfrak{B}}\}}
of
\ell^{2}(\Gamma)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}
. Conclusion 3.
3a
in Theorem
2.18
is just the abstract property of injective
C^{*}
-algebra morphisms, while Conclusion 3.
3b
follows from the results in Subsections
4.4
and
3.4
.

Section
6
is dedicated to the proof of Theorem
2.22
concerning the special case of a regular perturbing magnetic field having a non-zero constant part which allows for a more refined description of the associated effective Hamiltonian.

We have attached three appendices recalling a few basic facts about the Bloch-Floquet theory, the magnetic quantization and the theory of frames in Hilbert spaces. Let us notice that in the second appendix dedicated to the magnetic quantization we have also included some computational details of some arguments from Section
4
.

2
Precise formulation of the problem and the main result

2.1
Notations

We denote by
\mathbb{N}_{\bullet}:=\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
and for
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\bullet}
let
\underline{n}:=\{1,\ldots,n\}
. We use the convention
\mathbb{R}_{+}:=[0,+\infty)
. Given a normed linear space
\mathscr{V}
with the norm
|\cdot|
we use the notation
<v>:=\sqrt{1+|v|^{2}}
for
v\in\mathscr{V}
and respectively
v(\epsilon)=\mathscr{O}(\epsilon)
for a map
[0,\epsilon_{0}]\ni\epsilon\mapsto\,v(\epsilon)\in\mathscr{V}
, for some
\epsilon_{0}>0
, such that
v(0)=0
and
\epsilon^{-1}|v(\epsilon)|\leq C<\infty
for any
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0}]
.
We denote by
{\rm{1}\hskip-3.0pt\mathbf{l}}
the identity operator on any vector space, that we may sometimes indicate by an index;
I
I
d
will denote the identity map on any set, that we may also sometimes indicate by an index. For a subset
M
of a topological space we denote by
\mathring{M}
its interior subset (i.e. the largest open subset contained in
M
). Given two topological vector spaces
\mathscr{V}_{1}
and
\mathscr{V}_{2}
we denote by
\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{V}_{1};\mathscr{V}_{2})
the linear space of linear continuous maps
\mathscr{V}_{1}\rightarrow\mathscr{V}_{2}
with the topology of uniform convergence on bounded sets. Given any complex Hilbert space
\big(\mathcal{H},(\cdot,\cdot)_{\mathcal{H}}\big)
, the scalar product will be considered anti-linear in the first factor; we shall use the notations
\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H})
for the bounded linear operators on
\mathcal{H}
, respectively
\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{H})
for the finite-rank operators,
\mathbb{U}(\mathcal{H})
for the unitary operators,
\mathbb{L}(\mathcal{H})
for its orthogonal projections and
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{H})
for the family of 1-dimensional orthogonal projections.

Given a densely defined closable operator
T
acting in
\mathcal{H}
we denote by
\overline{T}
its closure. Given a self-adjoint operator
T
acting in a Hilbert space
{\cal H}
we denote by
E_{J}(T)
the spectral projection of
T
associated with the Borel subset
J\subset\mathbb{R}
.

We recall that a Fréchet space is a linear space endowed with a topology defined by a countable family of semi-norms
\{{\nu}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{\bullet}}
, for which it is complete. A bounded subset of a Fréchet space
\mathscr{V}
is a subset
M\subset\mathscr{V}
such that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\bullet}
there exists
C_{k}(M)>0
for which:

{\nu}_{k}(v)\leq\,C_{k}(M),\ \forall v\in M.

We shall freely use the notations introduced in Appendix
B
in connection with the magnetic pseudo-differential calculus with various magnetic fileds.

2.2
The framework

We shall work in dimension
d\geq 2
and denote by
\mathcal{X}\cong\mathbb{R}^{d}
the
’configuration space’
. In order to easily distinguish it from the
’momentum variables’
living in the dual of the configuration space, we shall use the notation
\mathcal{X}\cong\mathbb{R}^{d}
for the first one and
\mathcal{X}^{*}\cong\mathbb{R}^{d}
for the second one.
Denoting by
\big\{{\cal{e}}_{1},\ldots,{\cal{e}}_{d}\big\}
the canonical orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{X}\cong\mathbb{R}^{d}
we are given a discrete, regular lattice
\Gamma:=\underset{1\leq j\leq d}{\bigoplus}\mathbb{Z}{\cal{e}}_{j}\subset\mathcal{X}
.

Our operators are defined as
magnetic quantizations
using the extension of the Weyl quantization procedure introduced in
[
52
,
41
]
and very briefly recalled in Appendix
B
.

The phase space.

We will denote by
\Xi:=\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}^{*}
the phase space and by
<\cdot,\cdot>:\mathcal{X}^{*}\times\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
the canonical bilinear duality. We shall use systematically notations of the form
X:=(x,\xi)\in\Xi
,
Y:=(y,\eta)\in\Xi
, and so on.
We shall also consider the unique real scalar product
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\ni(x,y)\mapsto x\cdot y\in\mathbb{R}
making
\{{\cal{e}}_{1},\ldots,{\cal{e}}_{d}\}
an orthonormal basis. On the dual
\mathcal{X}^{*}
we shall introduce the
dual basis

\big\{{\cal{e}}^{*}_{j},\,j\in\underline{d}\big\}
defined by the relations
<{\cal{e}}^{*}_{j}\,,\,{\cal{e}}_{k}>=2\pi\delta_{jk}
for any pair
(j,k)\in\underline{d}\times\underline{d}
. We denote by
dx
, resp.
d\xi
the usual Lebesque measures on
\mathcal{X}
, resp.
\mathcal{X}^{*}
, associated with the above defined orthogonal basis. We recall the natural action by translations of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
on functions or distributions defined on
\mathcal{X}
, denoting by
\tau_{x}
the translation with
x\in\mathcal{X}
, resp. by translations on functions or distributions defined on
\mathcal{X}^{*}
, denoting by
\tau_{\xi}
for translations with
\xi\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
.

We have chosen to simplify some formulas by inserting the factor
2\pi
in the duality map
<\cdot,\cdot>:\mathcal{X}^{*}\times\mathcal{X}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
and thus for a decomposition
\xi=\underset{1\leq j\leq d}{\sum}\xi_{j}{\cal{e}}^{*}_{j}
we have the equality
<\xi,x>=2\pi\underset{1\leq j\leq d}{\sum}\xi_{j}\,x_{j}
. This leads to some modifications in the formula of the Fourier unitary operators and the fact that the elementary cells in
\Gamma
and
\Gamma_{*}
have both volume 1.

Using the decomposition
\mathcal{X}\ni{x}\mapsto\iota(x)+\hat{x}\in\Gamma\times[-1/2,1/2)^{d}
defined by
\iota(x)_{j}:=\lfloor x_{j}\rfloor:=\max\{k\in\mathbb{Z},\ k\leq x_{j}\}\in\mathbb{Z}
and the similar one
\mathcal{X}^{*}\ni\xi\mapsto\iota^{*}(\xi)+\hat{\xi}\in\Gamma_{*}\times[-1/2,1/2)^{d}
we shall consider
the unit cells:

\begin{split}\mathcal{E}:=\{\hat{x}=\underset{1\leq j\leq d}{\sum}\,\hat{x}_{j}\,{\cal{e}}_{j},\ -1/2\leq\hat{x}_{j}<1/2\}\subset\mathcal{X},\\
\mathcal{B}:=\{\hat{\xi}=\underset{1\leq j\leq d}{\sum}\,\hat{\xi}_{j}\,{\cal{e}}^{*}_{j},\ -1/2\leq\hat{\xi}_{j}<1/2\}\subset\mathcal{X}^{*}.\end{split}

(2.1)

We use the Hörmander multi-index notation
\partial^{\alpha}_{x}:=\partial_{x_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdot\ldots\cdot\partial_{x_{d}}^{\alpha_{d}}
and
|\alpha|:=\alpha_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{d}
for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
. For any
j\in\underline{d}
we denote by
\varepsilon_{j}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
the multi-index with components
(\varepsilon_{j})_{k}=\delta_{jk}
.

Some classes of functions.

Given a finite dimensional real Euclidean space
\mathcal{V}
with Euclidean norm denoted by
|\cdot|
let us consider the function spaces
BC^{\infty}(\mathcal{V})
of smooth complex functions on
\mathcal{V}
bounded together with all their derivatives,
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathcal{V})
of smooth and compactly supported complex functions on
\mathcal{V}
and
C^{\infty}_{\text{\tt pol}}(\mathcal{V})
of smooth complex functions on
\mathcal{V}
, with polynomial growth at infinity together with all their derivatives.
We define the weight functions (having the properties of a norm but allowed to take also the value
+\infty
) on
C^{\infty}(\mathcal{V})
:

\forall(p,n)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{N},\quad{\nu}_{p,n}(\phi):=\underset{y\in\mathcal{V}}{\sup}\,<y>^{p}\,\underset{|a|\leq n}{\max}\,\big|\big(\partial^{a}\phi\big)(y)\big|,\ \forall\phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{V}).

Then
BC^{\infty}(\mathcal{V})
is a Fréchet space for the familly of norms
\big\{{\nu}_{0,n}\big\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and we also define the space of Schwartz test functions:

\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{V})\,:=\,\big\{\phi\in BC^{\infty}(\mathcal{V})\,,\,{\nu}_{p,n}(\phi)<\infty,\,\forall(p,n)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{N}\big\}.

We denote by
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathcal{V})
the space of tempered distributions, defined as the topological dual of
\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{V})
on which we can consider either the weak topology with respect to
\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{V})
or the strong dual topology defined as the topology of uniform convergence on bounded sets of
\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{V})
. We denote by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{V}}:\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathcal{V})\times\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{V})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
the bilinear canonical duality map.

Notation 2.1
.

We shall denote by:

•

\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\Xi)_{\Gamma}
the tempered distributions on
\Xi
that are
\Gamma
-periodic with respect to the variable
x\in\mathcal{X}
. Similarly
C^{\infty}_{\Gamma}(\mathcal{X})
is the space of smooth,
\Gamma
-periodic functions on
\mathcal{X}\cong\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

•

\mathring{\mathscr{S}}_{\Delta}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X})\subset{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X})
with off-diagonal rapid decay together with all their derivatives. It has a natural Fréchet topology.

For a distribution
\mathfrak{K}\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X})
we denote by
\mathfrak{I}\mathit{nt}\,\mathfrak{K}\in\mathcal{L}\big(\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{X});\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathcal{X})\big)
the linear operator defined as:

\langle\big(\mathfrak{I}\mathit{nt}\,\mathfrak{K}\big)\phi,\psi\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}:=\langle\mathfrak{K},\phi\otimes\psi\rangle_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}}\mbox{ for any }(\phi,\psi)\in\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{X})\times\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{X})\,.

(2.2)

We use the notations
\mathfrak{I}\mathit{nt}\,\mathfrak{K}^{\dagger}:=\big(\mathfrak{I}\mathit{nt}\,\mathfrak{K}\big)^{*}
, so that
\langle\mathfrak{K}^{\dagger},\phi\otimes\psi\rangle_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}}=\overline{\langle\mathfrak{K},\psi\otimes\phi\rangle}_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}}
and
\mathfrak{I}\mathit{nt}\big(\mathfrak{K}\diamond\mathfrak{K}^{\prime}\big):=\big(\mathfrak{I}\mathit{nt}\,\mathfrak{K}\big)\cdot\big(\mathfrak{I}\mathit{nt}\,\mathfrak{K}^{\prime}\big)
.

For the
d
-dimensional Fourier transform we use the definitions:

\begin{split}&\big(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}}f\big)(\xi):=\int_{\mathcal{X}}\hskip-5.0ptdx\,e^{-i<\xi,x>}\,f(x)=\int_{\mathcal{X}}\hskip-5.0ptdx\,\exp\Big(-2\pi i\underset{j\in\underline{d}}{\sum}\xi_{j}\,x_{j}\Big)\,f(x),\ \forall f\in L^{1}(\mathcal{X})\\
&\big(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}^{*}}g\big)(x):=\int_{\mathcal{X}^{*}}\hskip-7.0ptd\xi\,e^{i<\xi,x>}\,g(\xi)=\int_{\mathcal{X}^{*}}\hskip-5.0ptd\xi\,\exp\Big(2\pi i\underset{j\in\underline{d}}{\sum}\xi_{j}\,x_{j}\Big)\,g(\xi),\ \forall g\in L^{1}(\mathcal{X}^{*}).\end{split}

(2.3)

The Hörmander classes of symbols.

Given
p\in\mathbb{R}
and
\rho\in\{0,1\}
let us define:

\begin{split}\qquad\qquad S^{p}_{\rho}(\Xi)\,:&=\,\big\{F\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\tt pol}}(\Xi)\,,\,\nu^{p,\rho}_{n,m}(F)<\infty,\,\forall(n,m)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\big\},\\
\text{where:}\qquad\qquad\qquad&\\
&\nu^{p,\rho}_{n,m}(F):=\underset{|\alpha|\leq n}{\max}\,\underset{|\beta|\leq m}{\max}\,\underset{(x,\xi)\in\Xi}{\sup}<\xi>^{-p+\rho m}\big|\big(\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}F\big)(x,\xi)\big|\,.\qquad\qquad\qquad\end{split}

We also define:

S^{\infty}_{\rho}(\Xi)\,:=\,\underset{p\in\mathbb{R}}{\bigcup}S^{p}_{\rho}(\Xi),\quad{S}^{-}_{\rho}(\Xi)\,:=\,\underset{p<0}{\bigcup}S^{p}_{\rho}(\Xi),\quad{S}^{-\infty}(\Xi)\,:=\,\underset{p\in\mathbb{R}}{\bigcap}S^{p}_{\rho}(\Xi).

Given
\Gamma\subset\mathcal{X}
as above, we shall denote by
S^{p}_{\rho}(\Xi)_{\Gamma}
the symbols of class
S^{p}_{\rho}(\Xi)
that are periodic with respect to
\Gamma
in the
x
-variable.
We shall use the following family of
\mathcal{X}
independent symbols
\mathfrak{m}_{s}(x,\xi):=<\xi>^{s}
, with
s\in\mathbb{R}
.

Definition 2.2
.

For
p>0
, a symbol
F\in S^{p}_{\rho}(\Xi)
is called elliptic, if there exists positive constants
c
and
R
such that, for
(x,\xi)\in\mathcal{X}
s.t.
|\xi|\geq R
,

|F(x,\xi)\big|\,\geq\,c<\xi>^{p}.

For any
s\geq 0
we consider
the Sobolev space of order

s
defined by:

\mathscr{H}^{s}(\mathcal{X})\,:=\,\big\{f\in{L}^{2}(\mathcal{X})\big|\ \mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}}\mathfrak{m}_{s}f\in{L}^{2}(\mathcal{X}^{*})\big\}.

The magnetic field.

Let us denote by
{{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}^{p}(\mathcal{X})
the real space of smooth
p
-forms on
\mathcal{X}
, by
{{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}_{\text{\tt bd}}^{p}(\mathcal{X})
those having components of class
BC^{\infty}(\mathcal{X})
and by
{{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}_{\text{\tt pol}}^{p}(\mathcal{X})
those with polynomial growth together with all their derivatives; let
d:{{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}^{p}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow{{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}^{p+1}(\mathcal{X})
be the exterior derivation.

A smooth magnetic field is an element
B\in{{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}^{2}(\mathcal{X})
that is closed, i.e. satisfies
dB=0
. Due to the contractibility of our affine space
\mathcal{X}
any magnetic field
B\in{{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}_{\text{\tt pol}}^{2}(\mathcal{X})
allows for a vector potential
A\in{{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}_{\text{\tt pol}}^{1}(\mathcal{X})
satisfying the equality
B=dA
and we shall always use such a choice.
We shall work with fields
B\in{{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}_{\text{\tt bd}}^{2}(\mathcal{X})
verifying the closure condition:
dB=0
, that we call
regular magnetic fields
. Given a magnetic field
B=dA
we use the following notations for the imaginary exponentials of the invariant integrals of
p
-forms on
p
-simplices:

\Lambda^{A}(x,y):=\exp\Big(-i\int_{[x,y]}A\Big),\qquad\Omega^{B}(x,y,z):=\exp\Big(-i\int_{<x,y,z>}B\Big).

Stokes’ Theorem clearly implies the formula:

\Omega^{B}(x,y,z)\,=\,\Lambda^{A}(x,y)\,\Lambda^{A}(y,z)\,\Lambda^{A}(z,x).

Definition 2.3
.

The magnetic Sobolev space of order
s\geq 0
and vector potential
A\in{{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}_{\text{\tt pol}}^{1}(\mathcal{X})
is the weighted Hilbert space:

\mathscr{H}^{s}_{A}(\mathcal{X})\,:=\,\big\{f\in{L}^{2}(\mathcal{X})\big|\ \mathfrak{Op}^{A}(\mathfrak{m}_{s})f\in{L}^{2}(\mathcal{X})\big\}

with
\mathfrak{Op}^{A}(\mathfrak{m}_{s})
considered as an operator in
\mathcal{L}\big(\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathcal{X})\big)
.

The
d
-dimensional torus.

An essential role in dealing with periodic problems is played by the
d
-dimensional torus

\mathbb{R}^{d}/\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cong\mathbb{S}^{d}:=\big\{\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{C},\ |\mathfrak{z}|=1\big\}^{d}\,.

(2.4)

When dealing with the identification
\mathcal{X}\cong\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\Gamma\cong\mathbb{Z}^{d}
we denote the quotient torus
\mathcal{X}/\Gamma
by
\mathbb{T}\cong\mathbb{S}^{d}
, while for the dual space
\mathcal{X}^{*}
with its dual lattice
\Gamma_{*}
we use the notation
\mathcal{X}^{*}/\Gamma_{*}=:\mathbb{T}_{*}\cong\mathbb{S}^{d}
and denote by
\theta\equiv(\mathfrak{z}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{z}_{d})
its points (with
\mathfrak{z}_{j}\in\mathbb{S}
for
1\leq j\leq d
). We mainly work with
\mathbb{T}_{*}
and consider on it the measure
d\theta
that is equal to the restriction of the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
with a normalization giving the total measure
1
to the manifold. With our definitions we get an explicit form for the canonical quotient projection:

\mathfrak{p}_{*}:\mathcal{X}^{*}\rightY\hskip-4.0pt\rightarrow\mathbb{T},\quad\mathfrak{p}_{*}\big(\underset{1\leq j\leq d}{\sum}\xi_{j}{\cal{e}}^{*}_{j}\big)\,=\,\big(e^{2\pi i\xi_{1}},\ldots,e^{2\pi i\xi_{d}}\big),\,\quad\forall(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,,

(2.5)

and we choose the following discontinuous injective section for it:

\mathfrak{s}_{*}:\mathbb{T}_{*}\rightarrow{\cal{B}}\subset\mathcal{X}^{*},\quad\mathfrak{s}_{*}(\mathfrak{z}_{1},\ldots\mathfrak{z}_{d}):=(2\pi)^{-1}(\arg\,\mathfrak{z}_{1},\ldots,\arg\,\mathfrak{z}_{d}),\quad\arg\mathfrak{z}\in[-\pi,\pi).

We recall that the irreducible unitary representations of the group
\Gamma\cong\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, that one calls its
characters
, form an abelian topological group that is isomorphic to
\mathbb{T}_{*}\cong\mathbb{S}^{d}
; given any
\theta=(\mathfrak{z}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{z}_{d})\in\mathbb{T}_{*}
its associated character is:

\chi_{\theta}:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{S},\quad\chi_{\theta}(\gamma):=e^{-i<\mathfrak{s}_{*}(\theta),\gamma>}.

(2.6)

2.3
The unperturbed Hamiltonian.

Hypothesis 2.4
.

h\in{S}^{p}_{1}(\Xi)_{\Gamma}
is a lower semi-bounded elliptic and
\Gamma
-periodic symbol for some
p>0
.

Hypothesis 2.5
.

B^{\circ}\in{{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}_{\text{\tt bd}}^{2}(\mathcal{X})
is a closed,
\Gamma
-periodic 2-form verifying the following zero-flux property:

\forall(j,k)\in\underline{d}\times\underline{d}:\quad\int\limits_{\text{\rm R}_{jk}}B^{\circ}=0,\quad\text{where}\quad\text{\rm R}_{jk}:=\big\{s\mathfrak{e}_{j}+t\mathfrak{e}_{k},(s,t)\in[0,1]^{2}\big\}.

(2.7)

Remark 2.6
.

In
[
37
]
the authors present an elegant cohomological argument proving that the zero-flux property (
2.7
) is a necessary and sufficient condition for the existence of a
\Gamma
-periodic vector potential
A^{\circ}\in{{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}_{\text{\tt bd}}^{1}(\mathcal{X})
such that
B^{\circ}=dA^{\circ}
. We shall always use such a choice of vector potential for
B^{\circ}
satisfying the zero-flux property.

Definition 2.7
.

For
h\in{S}^{p}_{1}(\Xi)_{\Gamma}
satisfying Hypothesis
2.4
,
B^{\circ}\in{{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}_{\text{\tt bd}}^{2}(\mathcal{X})
satisfying Hypothesis
2.5
and
A^{\circ}
chosen as in Remark
2.6
,
we call ”unperturbed Hamiltonian” the operator

H^{\circ}\,:=\,\overline{\mathfrak{Op}^{A^{\circ}}(h)}\,.

We shall work under the following hypothesis, that in fact brings no real loss of generality, as one can easily notice.

In fact, taking into account Proposition 2.2 in
[
17
]
one may notice that the inclusion of the regular magnetic field with periodic vector potential in Definition
2.7
does not necessitate to go outside the usual periodic Weyl calculus on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and there exists some symbol
h^{\circ}\in{S}^{p}_{1}(\Xi)_{\Gamma}
such that:

\mathfrak{Op}^{A^{\circ}}(h)\,=\,\mathfrak{Op}(h^{\circ}).

(2.8)

Remark 2.8
.

In
[
17
]
we also prove that
H^{\circ}
from Definition
2.7
is a lower semi-bounded, self-adjoint operator in
L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
, with domain
\mathscr{H}^{p}(\mathcal{X})
and commuting with all the translations
\tau_{\gamma}
with
\gamma\in\Gamma
that clearly leave invariant its domain.

Hypothesis 2.9
.

H^{\circ}\geq{E}_{0}{\rm{1}\hskip-3.0pt\mathbf{l}}
for some given
E_{0}>0
.

The Bloch-Floquet theory, that we recall very briefly in Appendix
A
, provides a unitary operator
\mathcal{U}_{BF}:L^{2}(\mathcal{X})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\mathscr{F}
, with
\mathscr{F}
the direct integral Hilbert space defined in (
A.1
) and (
A.5
), such that the decomposition:

\mathcal{U}_{BF}\,H^{\circ}\,\mathcal{U}_{BF}^{-1}=\int^{\oplus}_{{\mathbb{T}_{*}}}d\theta\Big(\underset{k\in\mathfrak{B}}{\sum}\lambda_{k}(\theta)\,\hat{\pi}_{k}(\theta)\Big)

(2.9)

has the properties described in Theorem
A.5
in the Appendix
A
(see details in
[
17
]
).

2.4
The isolated Bloch family.

Making use of the results in
[
17
]
and briefly recalled in Appendix
A
, for our periodic pseudo-differential operator
H^{\circ}
we shall suppose that:

Hypothesis 2.10
.

There exist
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}_{\bullet}
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that: (see figure
1
)

1.

\lambda_{k_{0}-1}(\theta)<\lambda_{k_{0}}(\theta),\quad\lambda_{k_{0}+N}(\theta)<\lambda_{k_{0}+N+1}(\theta),\quad\forall\theta\in\mathbb{T}_{*}\
, (by convention
\lambda_{0}:=-\infty
).

2.

E_{-}:=\underset{\theta\in\mathbb{T}_{*}}{\sup}\lambda_{k_{0}-1}(\theta)\,<\,E_{+}:=\underset{\theta\in\mathbb{T}_{*}}{\inf}\lambda_{k_{0}+N+1}(\theta),\quad\text{\tt d}_{0}:=E_{+}-E_{-}>0\,.

Brillouin zone
Energy
E_{+}
E_{+}^{\prime}
E_{-}^{\prime}
E_{-}
E_{0}
\lambda_{k_{0}}
\lambda_{k_{0}+N}
\lambda_{k_{0}-1}
\lambda_{k_{0}+N+1}

Figure 1:
Here
k_{0}=2
and
N=1
.

Definition 2.11
.

The set
\mathfrak{B}:=\big\{\lambda_{k}:\mathbb{T}_{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\,,\,k_{0}\leq k\leq k_{0}+N\big\}
satisfying Hypothesis
2.10
is called an isolated Bloch family of the Hamiltonian
H^{\circ}
. We
call
\mathfrak{B}
-operators the elements in
\mathbb{B}\big(P_{\mathfrak{B}}L^{2}(\mathcal{X})\big)
.

Note that under the above Hypothesis
2.10
, the interval
J_{\mathfrak{B}}:=(E_{-},E_{+})
is not empty.

Definition 2.12
.

Under Hypotheses
2.4
,
2.5
and
2.10
, using the results and notations in Appendix
A
we define:

•

the
\mathfrak{B}
-projection
:
P_{\mathfrak{B}}:=\mathfrak{U}_{BF}^{-1}\Big(\int^{\oplus}_{{\mathbb{T}_{*}}}d\theta\Big(\underset{k\in\mathfrak{B}}{\sum}\hat{\pi}_{k}(\theta)\Big)\mathfrak{U}_{BF}
,

•

the
\mathfrak{B}
-subspace
:
\mathscr{L}_{\mathfrak{B}}:=P_{\mathfrak{B}}L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
,

•

The
\mathfrak{B}
-Hamiltonian
:
H_{\mathfrak{B}}\,:=\,\mathfrak{U}_{BF}^{-1}\Big(\int^{\oplus}_{{\mathbb{T}_{*}}}d\theta\Big(\underset{k\in\mathfrak{B}}{\sum}\lambda_{k}(\theta)\,\hat{\pi}_{k}(\theta)\Big)\mathfrak{U}_{BF}\,\in\,\mathbb{B}\big(\mathscr{L}_{\mathfrak{B}}\big)
.

An important technical fact is Proposition
3.12
below in which we prove that there exists
p_{\mathfrak{B}}\in{S}^{-\infty}(\Xi)_{\Gamma}
and
h_{\mathfrak{B}}\in{S}^{-\infty}(\Xi)_{\Gamma}
such that
P_{\mathfrak{B}}=\mathfrak{Op}^{\circ}(p_{\mathfrak{B}})
and
H_{\mathfrak{B}}=\mathfrak{Op}^{\circ}(h_{\mathfrak{B}})
.

2.5
The perturbation.

Let us consider the self-adjoint operator
H^{\circ}
as defined in Definition
2.7
with the isolated Bloch family
\mathfrak{B}
as in Hypothesis
2.10
and let us add a perturbing magnetic field:

B(x)\,:=\,\epsilon{B}^{\epsilon}(x)\,=\,\epsilon\,{dA}^{\epsilon}(x)\,\equiv\,dA(x)

(2.10)

where
\epsilon\in[0,\epsilon_{0}]
for some
\epsilon_{0}>0
and the family
\mathbf{B}:=\{B^{\epsilon}\}_{\epsilon\in[0,\epsilon_{0}]}
is a bounded subset in
{{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}_{\text{\tt bd}}^{2}(\mathcal{X})
. Thus we have a total magnetic field

B^{\circ}(x)+B(x)=B^{\circ}(x)+\epsilon\,B^{\epsilon}(x)

with a vector potential
A^{\circ}(x)+A(x)=A^{\circ}(x)+\epsilon\,A^{\epsilon}(x)
in
{{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}_{\text{\tt pol}}^{1}(\mathcal{X})
.

Notation 2.13
.

In the sequel we shall frequenly use the notation
C(\mathbf{B})
for a strictly positive constant that depends on the bounded subset
\mathbf{B}
as defined after formula (
2.10
), more precisely on the supremum on the bounded subset
\mathbf{B}
of a finite set of continuous semi-norms on
{{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}_{\text{\tt bd}}^{2}(\mathcal{X})
.

Definition 2.14
.

The perturbed Hamiltonian is:

H^{\epsilon}\,:=\,\overline{\mathfrak{Op}^{A^{\circ}+A}(h)}
.

The following proposition, proven in
[
17
]
, gives the technical ingredient to deal with this 2-step quantization obtained by superposing the perturbing magnetic field
B
in (
2.10
) on the periodic magnetic field
B^{\circ}
from Hypothesis
2.5
.

Proposition 2.15
.

Suppose given a symbol
F\in{S}^{p}_{1}(\Xi)
for some
p\in\mathbb{R}
and two magnetic fields
B=dA
and
B^{\prime}=dA^{\prime}
in
{{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}_{\text{\tt bd}}^{2}(\mathcal{X})
with vector potentials
A\in{{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}_{\text{\tt pol}}^{1}(\mathcal{X})
and
A^{\prime}\in{{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}_{\text{\tt bd}}^{1}(\mathcal{X})
. Then:

\mathfrak{Op}^{A+A^{\prime}}(F)\,=\,\mathfrak{Op}^{A}\big(\mathfrak{S}^{A^{\prime}}[F]\big)\,,

with:

\mathfrak{S}^{A^{\prime}}[F]\,=\,({\rm{1}\hskip-3.0pt\mathbf{l}}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}})\big(\mathfrak{K}^{A^{\prime}}[F]\circ\Upsilon^{-1}\big)\,=\,({\rm{1}\hskip-3.0pt\mathbf{l}}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}})\big((\Lambda^{A^{\prime}}\mathfrak{K}[F])\circ\Upsilon^{-1}\big)\in{S}^{p}_{1}(\Xi)\,.

We shall use the following short notations:

\begin{split}\Lambda^{\epsilon}\,\equiv\,\Lambda^{A^{\circ}+\epsilon{A^{\epsilon}}},\ \widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}\,\equiv\,\Lambda^{\epsilon{A^{\epsilon}}},\ \mathfrak{Op}^{\epsilon}\,\equiv\,\mathfrak{Op}^{\epsilon{A^{\epsilon}}},\ \Omega^{\epsilon}\,\equiv\,\Omega^{B^{\circ}+\epsilon{B^{\epsilon}}},\ \widetilde{\Omega}^{\epsilon}\,\equiv\,\Omega^{\epsilon{B^{\epsilon}}},\ \sharp^{\epsilon}\,\equiv\,\sharp^{\epsilon{B^{\epsilon}}}.\end{split}

(2.11)

Remark 2.16
.

The above arguments imply that given any symbol
F\in{S}^{m}_{1}(\Xi)_{\Gamma}
, for any
m\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists a unique symbol
F^{\circ}\in{S}^{m}_{1}(\Xi)_{\Gamma}
such that
\mathfrak{Op}(F^{\circ})=\mathfrak{Op}^{A_{\circ}}(F)
and we also have the equalities:

\mathfrak{Op}^{A^{\circ}+\epsilon{A^{\epsilon}}}(h)=\mathfrak{Op}^{\epsilon}(h^{\circ}),\qquad\,H^{\epsilon}\,:=\,\overline{\mathfrak{Op}^{\epsilon}(h^{\circ})}.

(2.12)

Using the notations and results recalled in Appendix
B
we may prove the following statement.

Proposition 2.17
.

The operator
H^{\epsilon}
in Definition
2.14
has domain
\mathscr{H}^{p}_{A}(\mathcal{X})
and is a lower bounded self-adjoint operator in
L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
.

2.6
The main results.

Some spaces of matrices.

Given
M\in\mathbb{N}_{\bullet}
, we denote by
\mathscr{M}_{M}
the
C^{*}
-algebra of
M\times M
complex matrices. We shall use the notation
\mathscr{M}_{\Gamma}[\mathfrak{A}]
for the complex linear space of infinite matrices indexed by
\Gamma\times\Gamma
, having entries in a
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}
. Our main interest will be in the complex subspace
\mathscr{M}^{\circ}_{\Gamma}[\mathfrak{A}]
of matrices having rapid decay outside the diagonal; given any faithful representation
\rho:\mathfrak{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H})
we may view
\mathscr{M}^{\circ}_{\Gamma}[\mathfrak{A}]
as a sub-algebra of
\mathbb{B}\big(\ell^{2}(\Gamma;\mathcal{H})\big)
endowed with the operator norm that we denote by
\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{B}(\ell^{2}(\Gamma;\mathcal{H}))}
. We shall also deal with families of Toeplitz infinite matrices that we shall view as infinite sequences and use the notation:

{\cal{s}}\big(\Gamma;\mathfrak{A}\big):=\big\{\mathring{V}:\Gamma\rightarrow\,\mathfrak{A},\ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\ \underset{\gamma\in\Gamma}{\sup}<\gamma>^{n}\big\|\mathring{V}(\gamma)\big\|_{\mathfrak{A}}\hskip-5.69046pt<\infty\ \big\}.

Theorem 2.18
.

Consider the self-adjoint operator
H^{\circ}
from Definition
2.7
with the isolated Bloch family
\mathfrak{B}
as in Hypothesis
2.10
and its perturbed version
H^{\epsilon}:\mathscr{H}^{p}_{A}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow{L}^{2}(\mathcal{X})
, as in Definition
2.14
with a perturbing magnetic field
B=dA
as in (
2.10
). Then, there exists some
\epsilon_{0}>0
such that the following statements are true.

1.

There exist an orthogonal projection
P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}=\mathfrak{Op}^{\epsilon}(p^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}})\in\mathbb{B}\big(L^{2}(\mathcal{X})\big)
, a constant
C>0
and for any continuous semi-norm
{\nu}
on
{S}^{-\infty}(\Xi)
some constant
C_{\nu}>0
such that for any
\epsilon\in[0,\epsilon_{0}]
:

p^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\in{S}^{-\infty}(\Xi),\ \|[\,H^{\epsilon}\,,\,P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\,]\|\,\leq\,C{\epsilon},\ \text{and }{\nu}(p^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}-p_{\mathfrak{B}})\leq{C}_{\nu}\epsilon\,,

with
\mathfrak{Op}(p_{\mathfrak{B}})=P_{\mathfrak{B}}
given in Definition
2.12
.

2.

If we define the
reduced perturbed Hamiltonian
associated with
\mathfrak{B}
by

\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}:=P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}H^{\epsilon}{P}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\,,

then
\mathfrak{H}^{0}_{\mathfrak{B}}=H_{\mathfrak{B}}
given in Definition
2.12
and
there exists
\delta_{0}>0
such that for any
\delta\in[0,\delta_{0}]
the interval
J^{\delta}_{\mathfrak{B}}:=\big(E_{-}+{2}\delta\,,\,E_{+}-{2}\delta\big)
is non empty and there exists
\epsilon_{\delta}\in(0,\epsilon_{0}]
and
C>0
such that for any
\epsilon\in[0,\epsilon_{\delta}]
we have that:

(a)

\max\Big\{\underset{\lambda\in\sigma(\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}})\cap{J^{\delta}_{\mathfrak{B}}}}{\sup}{\rm dist}\Big(\lambda,\sigma(H^{\epsilon})\Big)\,,\,\underset{\lambda\in\sigma(H^{\epsilon})\cap\,J^{\delta}_{\mathfrak{B}}}{\sup}{\rm dist}\Big(\lambda,\sigma(\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}})\Big)\Big\}\,\leq\,C\epsilon^{2}\,.

(b)

For any
v\in E_{J}(H^{\epsilon})\,L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
:

\big\|\exp\big(-itH^{\epsilon}\big)v-\exp\big(-it\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\big)v\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}\leq\,C\,\big[\epsilon\,+\,(1+|t|)^{3}\,\epsilon^{2}\big]\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}.

3.

There exists
n_{\mathfrak{B}}\in\{N+1,\dots,N+1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor\}

1
1

1

we recall that
\lfloor d/2\rfloor:=\max\{k\in\mathbb{N},\ k\leq(d/2)\}
and an injective
C^{*}
-algebra morphism
\mathfrak{M}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}:\mathbb{B}\big(P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}L^{2}(\mathcal{X})\big)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{\circ}_{\Gamma}[\mathscr{M}(n_{\mathfrak{B}})]
such that:

(a)

\sigma(\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}})\,=\,\sigma(\mathfrak{M}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}])\setminus\{0\}
and
\mathfrak{M}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\big[\exp\big(-it\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\big)\big]=\exp\big(-it\mathfrak{M}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}]\big)
.

(b)

There exists
\mathring{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\widehat{H}_{\mathfrak{B}}]\in\mathcal{s}\big(\Gamma;\mathscr{M}(n_{\mathfrak{B}})\big)
independent of
\epsilon\in[0,\epsilon_{0}]
such that:

\big[\mathfrak{M}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}]\big]_{\alpha,\beta}=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}(\alpha,\beta)[\mathring{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\widehat{H}_{\mathfrak{B}}]]_{\alpha-\beta}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon),\quad{\forall\epsilon\in[0,\epsilon_{0}]}.

(2.13)

Remark 2.19
.

A more explicit form of the remainder in Formula (
2.13
) is obtained in Subsection
4.4
and Appendix
B.3.1
(see formula (
B.15
)).

Let us also formulate a version of Theorem
2.18
that gives a more detailed description of the
effective Hamiltonian

\mathfrak{M}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}]\in\mathscr{M}^{\circ}_{\Gamma}[\mathscr{M}(n_{\mathfrak{B}})]
when the perturbing magnetic field in (
2.10
) is a weak fluctuation around a non-zero constant field.

Hypothesis 2.20
.

Given a constant magnetic field
B^{\bullet}=dA^{\bullet}
, the perturbing magnetic field is of the form:

B(x)\,:=\,\epsilon\big(B^{\bullet}\,+\,\mathcal{c}\,B^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}(x)\big)

(2.14)

with
\big\{B^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}},\ (\epsilon,\mathcal{c})\in[0,\epsilon_{0}]\times[0,1]\big\}
a bounded set in
{{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}_{\text{\tt bd}}^{2}(\mathcal{X})
,
\epsilon\in[0,\epsilon_{0}]
as above and
\mathcal{c}\in[0,1]
a new ’small parameter’ controlling the weak fluctuations
\mathcal{c}\epsilon\,B^{\epsilon}\in{{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}_{\text{\tt bd}}^{2}(\mathcal{X})
.

Remark 2.21
.

As any magnetic field satisfying Hypothesis
2.20
is of the form (
2.10
) we deduce that Theorem
2.18
is true for the perturbed Hamiltonian defined with such a magnetic field, that we shall denote by
H^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}
. Suppose that it verifies Hypothesis
2.10
having the isolated Bloch family
\mathfrak{B}
. In order to keep in mind the special structure of the perturbing magnetic field we shall use the modified notations:
P^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
and
\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
instead of
P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}
and
\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}
for the operators appearing in Theorem
2.18
with
H^{\epsilon}
replaced by
H^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}
.

Under Hypothesis
2.20
, the third statement in Theorem
2.18
may be replaced by the following more detailed statement:

Theorem 2.22
.

Under the same hypothesis as in Theorem
2.18
, let us suppose that the perturbing magnetic field in (
2.10
) verifies Hypothesis
2.20
. Let us denote by
H^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}
the perturbed Hamiltonian defined as in Definition
2.14
with the magnetic field as above. Then for any
(\epsilon,\mathcal{c})\in[0,\epsilon_{0}]\times[0,1]
there exists an injective
C^{*}
-algebra morphism
\mathfrak{M}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}:\mathbb{B}\big(P^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}L^{2}(\mathcal{X})\big)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{\circ}_{\Gamma}[\mathscr{M}(n_{\mathfrak{B}})]
and a sequence
\mathring{\mathfrak{m}}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\in\mathcal{s}\big(\Gamma;\mathscr{M}(n_{\mathfrak{B}})\big)
such that points 3.
3a
and 3.
3b
in Theorem
2.18
remain true for
\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}
replaced by
\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
and (
2.13
) is replaced by the following equality:

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\big[\mathfrak{M}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}]_{\alpha,\beta}\big]_{p,q}\hskip-6.0pt=[\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon,0}](\alpha,\beta)\Big[\big[\mathring{\mathfrak{m}}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\widehat{H}_{\mathfrak{B}}]_{\alpha-\beta}\big]_{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p,q}\hskip-6.0pt+\mathcal{c}\epsilon\big[\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}]_{\alpha,\beta}\big]_{p,q}\Big]

(2.15)

where:

\mathring{\mathfrak{m}}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\widehat{H}_{\mathfrak{B}}]_{\gamma}=\mathring{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\widehat{H}_{\mathfrak{B}}]_{\gamma}+\epsilon\,\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\mathfrak{K}(\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}})]_{\beta+\gamma,\beta}

and

\epsilon\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\mathfrak{K}(\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}})]_{\alpha,\beta}

is the remainder in Formula (
2.13
) for the case
B=\epsilon{B}^{\bullet}
.

3
The Parseval frame of the isolated Bloch family

In order to study the properties of the isolated Bloch family projector
P_{\mathfrak{B}}
and define its “magnetic” version, we shall make use of Parseval frames (the reader may find some basic facts about frames in Hilbert spaces in Appendix
C
and more details in
[
9
]
or references therein). Starting with this section we shall constantly use the notations and definitions in the Appendices.

3.1
The decomposition of the Hamiltonian induced by the isolated Bloch family

Let us begin by emphasizing some consequences of Hypothesis
2.10
on the structure of the unperturbed operator
H^{\circ}
in Definition
2.7
, that play a crucial role in our arguments.
Theorem
A.5
in Appendix
A
and Hypothesis
2.10
allow us to define the following orthogonal decomposition:

\displaystyle{\rm{\displaystyle 1}\hskip-3.0pt\mathbf{l}}_{\mathcal{H}}=P_{0}\,\oplus\,P_{\mathfrak{B}}\,\oplus\,P_{\infty},\quad\text{given by:}

(3.1)

\displaystyle P_{0}:=\mathcal{U}_{BF}^{-1}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{T}_{*}}^{\oplus}d\theta\,\widehat{P_{0}}(\theta)\Big]\mathcal{U}_{BF},\quad\widehat{P_{0}}(\theta):=\underset{1\leq k\leq k_{0}-1}{\sum}\hat{\pi}_{k}(\theta)

\displaystyle P_{\mathfrak{B}}:=\mathcal{U}_{BF}^{-1}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{T}_{*}}^{\oplus}d\theta\,\widehat{P_{\mathfrak{B}}}(\theta)\Big]\mathcal{U}_{BF},\quad\widehat{P_{\mathfrak{B}}}(\theta):=\underset{k_{0}\leq k\leq k_{0}+N}{\sum}\hat{\pi}_{k}(\theta)

\displaystyle P_{\infty}:=\mathcal{U}_{BF}^{-1}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{T}_{*}}^{\oplus}d\theta\,\widehat{P_{\infty}}(\theta)\Big]\mathcal{U}_{BF},\quad\widehat{P_{\infty}}(\theta):=\underset{k_{0}+N+1\leq k}{\sum}\hat{\pi}_{k}(\theta)

and an associated decomposition of the Hamiltonian:
H^{\circ}\,=\,H_{0}\,\oplus\,H_{\mathfrak{B}}\,\oplus\,H_{\infty}
given explicitely by:

\displaystyle H_{0}:=\mathcal{U}_{BF}^{-1}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{T}_{*}}^{\oplus}d\theta\,\widehat{H_{0}}(\theta)\Big]\mathcal{U}_{BF},\quad\widehat{H_{0}}(\theta):=\underset{1\leq k\leq k_{0}-1}{\sum}\lambda_{k}(\theta)\,\hat{\pi}_{k}(\theta),

(3.2)

\displaystyle H_{\mathfrak{B}}:=\mathcal{U}_{BF}^{-1}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{T}_{*}}^{\oplus}d\theta\,\widehat{H_{\mathfrak{B}}}(\theta)\Big]\mathcal{U}_{BF},\quad\widehat{H_{\mathfrak{B}}}(\theta):=\underset{k_{0}\leq k\leq k_{0}+N}{\sum}\lambda_{k}(\theta)\,\hat{\pi}_{k}(\theta),

(3.3)

\displaystyle H_{\infty}:=\mathcal{U}_{BF}^{-1}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{T}_{*}}^{\oplus}d\theta\,\widehat{H_{\infty}}(\theta)\Big]\mathcal{U}_{BF}\quad\widehat{H_{\infty}}(\theta):=\overline{\underset{k_{0}+N+1\leq k}{\sum}\lambda_{k}(\theta)\,\hat{\pi}_{k}(\theta)}.

(3.4)

Using Hypotheses
2.4
,
2.9
and
2.10
we notice that (for
E_{0}\leq E_{-}<E_{+}
as defined in this last Hypothesis):

\sigma(H_{0})\subset\{0\}\,\bigsqcup\,[E_{0},E_{-}],\quad\sigma(H_{\mathfrak{B}})\subset\{0\}\bigsqcup\,[E_{-}^{\prime},E_{+}^{\prime}],\quad\sigma(H_{\infty})\subset\{0\}\bigsqcup\,[E_{+},\infty),

for some
E^{\prime}_{-}
and
E^{\prime}_{+}
such that
0<E_{0}\leq E^{\prime}_{-}<E^{\prime}_{+}<\infty
. If we have the strict inequalities
E_{-}<E_{-}^{\prime}
and
E_{+}^{\prime}<E_{+}
we are in a situation as in Figure
3.1
where the isolated band consists of just one eigenvalue and it forms a spectral island for
H^{\circ}
, separated from the rest of the spectrum by two gaps. Then the three orthogonal projections in the above decomposition are in fact spectral projections of
H^{\circ}
associated with disjoint components of the spectrum
\sigma(H^{\circ})
so that the “global gap condition” is fulfilled and we are in the situation studied in
[
19
]
. Thus, we shall be mainly interested in the case:

0<E_{0}\leq E_{-}^{\prime}\leq E_{-}<E_{+}\leq E_{+}^{\prime}<\infty,

(3.5)

illustrated in Figure
1
, where we have an isolated band with two crossing Bloch levels in red and green. (Formally, the green colour should always be on top of the red colour because
\lambda_{k_{0}}\leq\lambda_{k_{0}+N}
, but we will never treat them individually, only as a well-defined isolated family.) The energy interval we are interested in is
(E_{-},E_{+})
where
E_{-}
is the maximum of the blue eigenvalue
\lambda_{k_{0}-1}
and
E_{+}
is the minimum of the the black one
\lambda_{k_{0}+N+1}
and the Hamiltonian
H^{\circ}
does not have a spectral gap and the three orthogonal projections in (
3.1
) are not necessarily spectral projections of
H^{\circ}
.

Brillouin zone
Energy
E_{+}
E_{+}^{\prime}
E_{-}^{\prime}
E_{-}
E_{0}
\lambda_{k_{0}}=\lambda_{k_{0}+N}
\lambda_{k_{0}-1}
\lambda_{k_{0}+N+1}

Figure 2:
Here
k_{0}=2
and
N=0
.

We emphasize that the non-zero spectrum of the fibre of
H_{\mathfrak{B}}
is always well isolated from the other Bloch bands which build up
H_{0}
and
H_{\infty}
.

3.2
The vector bundle associated with the Bloch-Floquet-Zak representation

We shall recall here the construction elaborated in
[
60
]
in order to have the necessary background to formulate our main technical ideas and go further with our analysis. The point is to build a vector bundle whose sections will be in bijective corespondence with the functions in the BFZ representation space
\mathscr{G}
in Appendix
A
. In fact we shall need to use this construction for several families of functions with specific regularity properties. More precisely we shall need to work with all the spaces in (
A.11
). Let us denote generically by
\mathscr{K}
any of the spaces
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})\subset\mathscr{H}^{p}(\mathbb{T})\subset{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T})
and recalling the definition of
\mathfrak{f}
before (
A.11
), let us consider the unitary representations:

\mathring{U}_{*}:\Gamma_{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{U}(\mathscr{K}),\qquad[\mathring{U}_{*}(\gamma^{*})\mathring{f}](\omega):=\mathfrak{f}_{-\gamma^{*}}\big(\mathfrak{s}(\omega)\big)\mathring{f}(\omega),\quad\forall\omega\in\mathbb{T}.

(3.6)

We define
\mathfrak{X}[\mathscr{K}]:=\mathcal{X}^{*}\times{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T})
, considering it as infinite dimensional smooth real manifold and consider the following equivalence relation on
\mathfrak{X}[\mathscr{K}]
induced by the unitary representation (
3.6
):

(\xi,\mathring{f})\,\text{\LARGE$\sim$}\,(\zeta,\mathring{g})\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\exists\gamma^{*}\in\Gamma^{*},\ \zeta=\xi+\gamma^{*},\ \mathring{g}=\mathring{U}_{*}(\gamma^{*})\mathring{f}.

(3.7)

Let us denote the family of equivalence classes by
\widehat{\mathfrak{X}}[\mathscr{K}]:=\mathfrak{X}[\mathscr{K}]/\text{\LARGE$\sim$}
and consider the surjection
\mathfrak{t}:\widehat{\mathfrak{X}}[\mathscr{K}]\ni[\xi,\mathring{f}]_{\sim}\mapsto\mathfrak{p}_{*}(\xi)\in\mathbb{T}_{*}
, that is well defined due to the fact that
(\xi,\mathring{f})\,\text{\LARGE$\sim$}\,(\zeta,\mathring{g})
implies that
\mathfrak{p}_{*}(\xi)=\mathfrak{p}_{*}(\zeta)
. We have the homeomorphisms:

\mathfrak{t}^{-1}(\theta)=\big\{[\mathfrak{s}_{*}(\theta),\mathring{f}],\ \mathring{f}\in\mathscr{K}\big\}\simeq\mathscr{K}

(3.8)

and
\mathfrak{t}^{-1}\mathcal{O}\simeq\mathcal{O}\times\mathscr{K}
for any small enough neighborhood
\mathcal{O}
of any
\theta\in\mathbb{T}_{*}
.

Notation 3.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}\big(\mathcal{O},\widehat{\mathfrak{X}}[\mathscr{K}]\big)
be the complex space of smooth sections over the open set
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{T}_{*}
and
\mathfrak{S}^{2}\big(\mathcal{O},\widehat{\mathfrak{X}}[\mathscr{K}]\big)
the complex space of classes of square integrable sections over the open set
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{T}_{*}
.

Proposition 3.2
.

We have an unitary map

\mathfrak{S}^{2}\big(\mathbb{T}_{*},\widehat{\mathfrak{X}}[\mathscr{K}]\big)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\big\{F\in{L}^{2}_{\text{\tt loc}}\big(\mathcal{X}^{*};\mathscr{K}\big),\ \tau_{\gamma^{*}}F=\mathfrak{f}_{-\gamma^{*}}F\big\}\,=:\,L^{2}_{l,\mathfrak{f}}[\mathscr{K}]\,,

(3.9)

that restricts to a bijection

\mathfrak{S}\big(\mathbb{T}_{*},\widehat{\mathfrak{X}}[\mathscr{K}]\big)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\big\{F\in{C}^{\infty}\big(\mathcal{X}^{*};\mathscr{K}\big),\ \tau_{\gamma^{*}}F=\mathfrak{f}_{-\gamma^{*}}F\big\}\,=:\,C^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{f}}[\mathscr{K}]\,.

(3.10)

Proof.

Suppose given
F\in C^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{f}}[\mathscr{K}]
and for
\theta\in\mathbb{T}_{*}
let us define

\Phi_{F}(\theta):=\big[\mathfrak{s}_{*}(\theta),(F\circ\mathfrak{s}_{*})(\theta)\big]_{\sim}\in\widehat{\mathfrak{X}}[\mathscr{K}]_{\theta}\subset\widehat{\mathfrak{X}}[\mathscr{K}]\,.

(3.11)

One easily verifies the smoothness properties using the smoothness of
F
and concludes that
\Phi_{F}\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathbb{T}_{*},\widehat{\mathfrak{X}}[\mathscr{K}])
.

Conversely, let
\Phi\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathbb{T}_{*},\widehat{\mathfrak{X}}[\mathscr{K}])
and given
\xi\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
let us notice that there exists a unique
\widetilde{\Phi}(\xi)\in\mathscr{K}
such that:

(\Phi\circ\mathfrak{p}_{*})(\xi)\,=\,\big[\xi,\widetilde{\Phi}(\xi)\big]_{\sim}.

(3.12)

In fact, using the decomposition
\mathcal{X}^{*}=\Gamma_{*}\times{\cal{B}}
with the notations discussed in Appendix
2.2
, there exists a unique
\varphi\in\mathscr{K}
such that we can write:

(\Phi\circ\mathfrak{p}_{*})(\xi)=\big\{\big(\hat{\xi}+\gamma^{*}\,,\,\mathfrak{f}_{-\gamma^{*}}\varphi\big)\big\}_{\gamma^{*}\in\Gamma_{*}}

(3.13)

and we obtain that

\widetilde{\Phi}(\xi):=\mathfrak{f}_{-\iota^{*}(\xi)}\varphi\in\mathscr{K}.

(3.14)

∎

We shall use the notations:

\mathfrak{F}:=\widehat{\mathfrak{X}}[L^{2}(\mathbb{T})],\quad\mathfrak{F}^{p}:=\widehat{\mathfrak{X}}[\mathscr{H}^{p}(\mathbb{T})],\quad\mathfrak{F}^{\infty}:=\widehat{\mathfrak{X}}[C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})].

(3.15)

3.3
The vector sub-bundle associated with the isolated Bloch family
\mathbf{\mathfrak{B}}
.

Proposition 3.3
.

There exists a vector sub-bundle
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathfrak{B}}\subset\mathfrak{F}^{\infty}\rightY\hskip-4.0pt\rightarrow\mathbb{T}_{*}
with an unitary map
\mathfrak{S}^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{*},\mathfrak{F}_{\mathfrak{B}})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{U}_{BFZ}\big[P_{\mathfrak{B}}{L}^{2}(\mathcal{X})\big]
putting into bijective correspondence the smooth sections in
\mathfrak{S}(\mathbb{T}_{*},\mathfrak{F}_{\mathfrak{B}})
with smooth functions in
\mathcal{U}_{BFZ}\big[P_{\mathfrak{B}}{L}^{2}(\mathcal{X})\big]\subset\mathscr{G}^{\infty}
.

Proof.

We begin by noticing that the formulas (
3.1
) imply that any vector in the range of
\hat{P}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\theta)
is a finite linear combination of elements in the ranges of the projections
\hat{\pi}_{k}(\theta)
with
k_{0}\leq k\leq k_{0}+N
and thus, Theorem
A.6
implies that they belong to
\mathscr{F}_{\theta}^{\infty}
. It follows that

\mathcal{U}_{BFZ}\big[P_{\mathfrak{B}}{L}^{2}(\mathcal{X})\big]\subset\mathscr{G}^{\infty}
.

Due to Hypothesis
1.1
, for any
\theta\in\mathbb{T}_{*}
we can find a cut-off function
\chi_{\theta}\in\,C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
with support in
\widetilde{I}_{\theta}:=(\lambda_{k_{0}-1}(\theta),\lambda_{k_{0}+N+1}(\theta)\neq\emptyset
and equal to 1 on
J_{\theta}:=[\lambda_{k_{0}}(\theta),\lambda_{k_{0}+N}(\theta)]
so that:

\widehat{P}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\theta)\,=\,\chi_{\theta}\big(\widehat{H}^{\circ}(\theta)\big).

Let us notice further that for any
\theta\in\mathbb{T}^{d}_{*}
, we can find an open neighbourhood
\mathscr{O}\subset\mathbb{T}^{d}_{*}
and a local smooth section
\mathfrak{s}_{\mathscr{O}}:\mathscr{O}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}^{*}
such that we may choose the same
\chi_{\theta}\equiv\chi_{\mathscr{O}}
for all
\theta\in\mathscr{O}
. Using the second statement of Theorem
A.5
we deduce the smoothness of the application:

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}_{\mathscr{O}}\mathscr{O}\ni\xi\mapsto\mathfrak{f}_{\xi}^{-1}\chi_{\mathscr{O}}\big(\widehat{H}^{\circ}\big(\mathfrak{p}_{*}(\xi)\big)\mathfrak{f}_{\xi}\in\mathbb{B}\big(L^{2}(\mathbb{T})\big).

(3.16)

Due to the compactness of
\mathbb{T}_{*}
it follows that we can find an open cover
\big\{\mathscr{O}_{m},\ m\in\underline{N}\big\}
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}_{\bullet}
and an associated finite partition of unity
\big\{\rho_{m}\in\,C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{T}_{*}),\ m\in\underline{N}\big\}
, as well as a family of functions
\chi_{m}\in\,C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}),\ m\in\underline{N}\big\}
such that
\chi_{m}=1
on
J_{\theta}
and
\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits\chi_{m}\subset\widetilde{I}_{\theta}
for any
\theta\in\mathscr{O}_{m}
. Finally, we notice that:

\widehat{P}_{\mathfrak{B}}\,=\,\underset{1\leq m\leq N}{\sum}\rho_{m}\,\chi_{m}\big(\widehat{H}^{\circ}\big)

(3.17)

and the following map is smooth:

\mathcal{X}^{*}\ni\xi\mapsto\widetilde{P}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\xi):=\mathfrak{f}_{\xi}^{-1}\widehat{P}_{\mathfrak{B}}\big(\mathfrak{p}_{*}(\xi)\big)\mathfrak{f}_{\xi}\in\mathbb{B}\big(L^{2}(\mathbb{T})\big).

(3.18)

Finally, our previous arguments show that
\widetilde{P}_{\mathfrak{B}}\mathscr{G}\subset\mathscr{G}^{\infty}
and for any
\tilde{f}\in\mathscr{G}
:

\begin{split}\big[\tau_{\gamma^{*}}\widetilde{P}_{\mathfrak{B}}\tilde{f}\big](\xi)=\mathfrak{f}_{\xi+\gamma*}^{-1}\widehat{P}_{\mathfrak{B}}\big(\mathfrak{p}_{*}(\xi+\gamma^{*})\big)\mathfrak{f}_{\xi+\gamma^{*}}\,\tilde{f}(\xi+\gamma^{*})=\mathfrak{f}_{\gamma*}^{-1}\widetilde{P}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\xi)\,\tilde{f}(\xi)=\big[\mathfrak{f}_{\gamma^{*}}^{-1}\widetilde{P}_{\mathfrak{B}}\tilde{f}\big](\xi).\end{split}

(3.19)

We conclude that we may take
\mathscr{K}:=\widetilde{P}_{\mathfrak{B}}(1)\big[C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})\big]
in the abstract construction done in the previous section and obtain the vector bundle
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathfrak{B}}
of rank
N+1
as sub-bundle of
\mathfrak{F}^{\infty}
.

∎

3.4
Construction of the “unperturbed” Parseval frame

The main property of fibre bundles that we shall need in our construction is the following Embedding Theorem (see Theorem 7.2 in Chapter 8 of
[
40
]
, we use the notation introduced in footnote
1
):

Theorem 3.4
.

Given a smooth Euclidean vector bundle
\mathfrak{B}\rightY\hskip-4.0pt\rightarrow M
of rank
n
over a smooth real manifold
M
of dimension
d
, there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
with

0\leq m\leq\lfloor d/2\rfloor

and a smooth isometric bundle homomorphism
\mathfrak{I}:\mathfrak{B}\rightarrow M\times\mathbb{C}^{n+m}
.

Applying the Embedding Theorem to our case where
n=N+1
and
M=\mathbb{T}_{*}
we have:

Corollary 3.5
.

There exist some
n_{\mathfrak{B}}\in\mathbb{N}_{\bullet}
with
N+1\leq n_{\mathfrak{B}}\leq\,{N+1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}
and a smooth bundle homomorphism
\mathfrak{I}_{\mathfrak{B}}
from
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathfrak{B}}
into
\mathbb{T}_{*}\times\mathbb{C}^{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}
that is isometric on each fibre.

Corollary 3.6
.

The application
\sigma\mapsto\mathfrak{I}_{\mathfrak{B}}\circ\sigma
defines an isometric map from
\mathfrak{S}^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{*},\mathfrak{F}_{\mathfrak{B}})
into
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{*};\mathbb{C}^{n_{\mathfrak{B}}})
and a continuous linear embedding
\mathfrak{S}(\mathbb{T}_{*},\mathfrak{F}_{\mathfrak{B}})\hookrightarrow\big[C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}_{*})\big]^{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}
.

Proposition 3.7
.

There exists a family of
n_{\mathfrak{B}}
smooth global sections
\{\hat{\psi}_{p}\}_{1\leq p\leq n_{\mathfrak{B}}}
in the Bloch bundle
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathfrak{B}}\rightY\hskip-4.0pt\rightarrow\mathbb{T}_{*}
such that the closed complex linear space they generate is equal to
\mathfrak{S}(\mathbb{T}_{*},\mathfrak{F}_{\mathfrak{B}})
and for any section
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{*},\mathfrak{F}_{\mathfrak{B}})
the following equalities hold:

\sigma(\theta)\,=\,\underset{1\leq p\leq n_{\mathfrak{B}}}{\sum}\,\big(\hat{\psi}_{p}(\theta)\,,\,\sigma(\theta)\big)_{\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{*}^{-1}(\theta)}\hat{\psi}_{p}(\theta),\quad\forall\theta\in\mathbb{T}_{*}\,,

with the identity

\big\|\sigma(\theta)\big\|_{\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{*}^{-1}(\theta)}^{2}\,=\,\underset{1\leq p\leq n_{\mathfrak{B}}}{\sum}\,\big|\big(\hat{\psi}_{p}(\theta)\,,\,\sigma(\theta)\big)_{\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{*}^{-1}(\theta)}\big|^{2},\quad\forall\theta\in\mathbb{T}_{*}\,.

Proof.

We notice that the bundle homomorphism
\mathfrak{I}_{\mathfrak{B}}:\mathfrak{F}_{\mathfrak{B}}\rightarrow\mathbb{T}_{*}\times\mathbb{C}^{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}
is in fact a smooth family of linear isometries
\mathbb{T}_{*}\ni\theta\mapsto\mathfrak{I}_{\mathfrak{B},\theta}\in\mathcal{L}\big(\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{*}^{-1}(\theta),\mathbb{C}^{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}\big)
.
For any
{\theta}\in\mathbb{T}_{*}
let:

\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{B}}({\theta}):=\big\{\mathfrak{I}_{\mathfrak{B},\theta}\mathfrak{v}\,,\,\mathfrak{v}\in\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{*}^{-1}(\theta)\big\}\,\subset\,\mathbb{C}^{n_{\mathfrak{B}}},

(3.20)

so that
\mathfrak{I}_{\mathfrak{B},\theta}:\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{*}^{-1}(\theta)\rightarrow\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{B}}({\theta})
is invertible and let
Q_{\mathfrak{B}}({\theta}):\mathbb{C}^{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{B}}({\theta})
be the canonical orthogonal projection defined by this subspace.
If
\big\{\mathcal{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{e}_{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}\big\}
is the canonical orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{C}^{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}
, we can define the following global sections:

\hat{\psi}_{p}({\theta}):=\mathfrak{I}_{\mathfrak{B},\theta}^{-1}\big(Q_{\mathfrak{B}}(\theta)\mathcal{e}_{p}\big),\quad\forall(p,{\theta})\in\underline{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}\times\mathbb{T}_{*}.

(3.21)

Given any
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathfrak{F}_{\mathfrak{B}},\mathbb{T}_{*})
we can write that:

\displaystyle\sigma(\theta)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{I}_{\mathfrak{B},\theta}^{-1}\big(Q_{\mathfrak{B}}(\theta)\mathfrak{I}_{\mathfrak{B},\theta}\sigma(\theta)\big)=\mathfrak{I}_{\mathfrak{B},\theta}^{-1}\Big(Q_{\mathfrak{B}}(\theta)\Big(\underset{1\leq p\leq n_{\mathfrak{B}}}{\sum}\big(\mathfrak{I}_{\mathfrak{B},\theta}\sigma(\theta)\,,\,\mathcal{e}_{p}\big)_{\mathbb{C}^{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}}\,\mathcal{e}_{p}\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\underset{1\leq p\leq n_{\mathfrak{B}}}{\sum}\big(\mathfrak{I}_{\mathfrak{B},\theta}\sigma(\theta)\,,\,\mathcal{e}_{p}\big)_{\mathbb{C}^{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}}\,\hat{\psi}_{p}(\theta)=\underset{1\leq p\leq n_{\mathfrak{B}}}{\sum}\big(Q_{\mathfrak{B}}(\theta)\mathfrak{I}_{\mathfrak{B},\theta}\sigma(\theta)\,,\,Q_{\mathfrak{B}}(\theta)\mathcal{e}_{p}\big)_{\mathbb{C}^{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}}\,\hat{\psi}_{p}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\underset{1\leq p\leq n_{\mathfrak{B}}}{\sum}\big(\sigma(\theta)\,,\,\hat{\psi}_{p}(\theta)\big)_{\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{*}^{-1}(\theta)}\,\hat{\psi}_{p}(\theta).

In a similar way we notice that:

\displaystyle\big\|\sigma(\theta)\big\|_{\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{*}^{-1}(\theta)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\big\|\mathfrak{I}_{\mathfrak{B},\theta}\sigma(\theta)\big\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}}^{2}=\underset{1\leq p\leq n_{\mathfrak{B}}}{\sum}\big|\big(Q_{\mathfrak{B}}(\theta)\mathfrak{I}_{\mathfrak{B},\theta}\sigma(\theta),\mathcal{e}_{p}\big)_{\mathbb{C}^{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}}\big|^{2}

\displaystyle=\underset{1\leq p\leq n_{\mathfrak{B}}}{\sum}\big|\big(\sigma(\theta),\hat{\psi}_{p}\big)_{\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{*}^{-1}(\theta)}\big|^{2}.

∎

Let us map our
n_{\mathfrak{B}}
global smooth sections obtained in Proposition
3.7
into
n_{\mathfrak{B}}
rapidly decaying smooth functions in
L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
by the inverse Bloch-Floquet transform:

\psi_{p}(\hat{x}+\gamma)\,:=\,\mathcal{U}_{BF}^{-1}\hat{\psi}_{p}\,=\,\int_{\mathbb{T}_{*}}d{\theta}\,e^{i<{\theta},\gamma>}\,\hat{\psi}_{p}(\hat{x};{\theta}).

(3.22)

They define a well-localized finite-dimensional complex subspace of the isolated Bloch family subspace
P_{\mathfrak{B}}\,L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
. Moreover, for any
\alpha\in\Gamma
, the translated functions
\tau_{\alpha}\psi_{p}
verify the equalities:

\big(\mathcal{U}_{BF}\tau_{\alpha}\psi_{p}\big)(\hat{x};{\theta})=\underset{\gamma\in\Gamma}{\sum}e^{-i<{\theta},\gamma>}\,\psi_{p}(\hat{x}+\gamma+\alpha)=e^{i<{\theta},\alpha>}\hat{\psi}_{p}(\hat{x};{\theta})\in\big[\widehat{P}_{\mathfrak{B}}({\theta})\,\mathscr{F}_{\theta}\big]

and we conclude that:

\text{\large{$\boldsymbol{\Psi}$}}_{\mathfrak{B}}:=\Big\{\tau_{\alpha}\,\psi_{p},\ \alpha\in\Gamma,\ 1\leq p\leq n_{\mathfrak{B}}\Big\}\,\subset\,P_{\mathfrak{B}}\,L^{2}(\mathcal{X}).

(3.23)

Proposition 3.8
.

The family
\text{\large{$\boldsymbol{\Psi}$}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
defines a Parseval frame for the subspace
P_{\mathfrak{B}}\,L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
associated with the isolated Bloch family
\mathfrak{B}
.

Proof.

We prove first that
P_{\mathfrak{B}}\,L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
is the linear span of the family
\text{\large{$\boldsymbol{\Psi}$}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
. Thus let us choose any
f\in P_{\mathfrak{B}}\,L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
and consider its Bloch-Floquet transform:

\hat{f}_{\theta}(\hat{x}):=\big(\mathcal{U}_{BF}f\big)(\hat{x},{\theta})=\big(\mathcal{U}_{BF}P_{\mathfrak{B}}f\big)(\hat{x},{\theta})=\hat{P}_{\mathfrak{B}}({\theta})\big[\hat{f}_{\theta}(\hat{x})\big]

that defines a bounded measurable global section
\mathbb{T}_{*}\ni\theta\mapsto\hat{f}_{\theta}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{E})\simeq\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{*}^{-1}(\theta)
and thus an element in
\mathfrak{S}^{2}(\mathfrak{F}_{\mathfrak{B}};\mathbb{T}_{*})
.
Using Proposition
3.7
we can write that:

\hat{f}_{\theta}=\underset{1\leq p\leq n_{\mathfrak{B}}}{\sum}\,\big(\hat{\psi}_{p}({\theta})\,,\,\hat{f}_{\theta}\big)_{\mathscr{F}_{\theta}}\hat{\psi}_{p}({\theta})

and consequently:

\displaystyle f(\hat{x}+\gamma)\,

\displaystyle=\,\big(\mathcal{U}_{BF}^{-1}\hat{f}\big)(\hat{x}+\gamma)=\int_{\mathbb{T}_{*}}d{\theta}\,e^{i<{\theta},\gamma>}\,\hat{f}_{\theta}(\hat{x})

\displaystyle=\underset{1\leq p\leq n_{\mathfrak{B}}}{\sum}\,\int_{\mathbb{T}_{*}}d{\theta}\,e^{i<{\theta},\gamma>}\,\big(\hat{\psi}_{p}({\theta})\,,\,\hat{f}_{\theta}\big)_{\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{*}^{-1}(\theta)}\hat{\psi}_{p}(\hat{x},{\theta})

\displaystyle=\,\underset{1\leq p\leq n_{\mathfrak{B}}}{\sum}\,\underset{\alpha\in\Gamma}{\sum}\,\big[\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{T}_{*}}\big(\hat{\psi}_{p}(\cdot)\,,\,\hat{f}\big)_{\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{*}^{-1}(\cdot)}\big](\alpha)\,\psi_{p}(\hat{x}+\gamma-\alpha).

For
(\alpha,p)\in\Gamma\times\underline{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}
we introduce the notation:

\mathfrak{C}_{\mathfrak{B}}(f)_{\alpha,p}:=\big[\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{T}_{*}}\big(\hat{\psi}_{p}(\cdot)\,,\,\hat{f}\big)_{\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{*}^{-1}(\cdot)}\big](\alpha)\,,

and conclude that:

f=\underset{1\leq p\leq n_{\mathfrak{B}}}{\sum}\,\underset{\alpha\in\Gamma}{\sum}\,\mathfrak{C}_{\mathfrak{B}}(f)_{\alpha,p}\,[\tau_{-\alpha}\psi_{p}].

(3.24)

In order to prove that it is a Parseval frame let us compute the scalar products
\big(\tau_{-\alpha}\psi_{p}\,,\,f\big)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}
for some
f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
and some indices
(\alpha,p)\in\Gamma\times\underline{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}
:

\displaystyle\big(\tau_{-\alpha}\psi_{p},f\big)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}

\displaystyle=\underset{\gamma\in\Gamma}{\sum}\int_{\mathcal{E}}d\hat{x}\,f(\hat{x}+\gamma)\,\overline{\psi_{p}(\hat{x}+\gamma-\alpha)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}_{*}}d{\theta}\,e^{i<{\theta},\alpha>}\int_{\mathcal{E}}d\hat{x}\,\hat{f}_{\theta}(\hat{x})\,\overline{\hat{\psi}_{p}(\hat{x},{\theta})}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}_{*}}d{\theta}\,e^{i<{\theta},\alpha>}\,\big(\hat{\psi}_{p}({\theta})\,,\,\hat{f}_{\theta}\big)_{\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{*}^{-1}(\theta)}=\mathfrak{C}_{\mathfrak{B}}(f)_{\alpha,p}\,.

Using this in (
3.24
) we finally obtain

f=\underset{1\leq p\leq n_{\mathfrak{B}}}{\sum}\,\underset{\alpha\in\Gamma}{\sum}\,\big(\tau_{-\alpha}\psi_{p},f\big)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}\,[\tau_{-\alpha}\psi_{p}]\,.

∎

Using the rapid decay of the functions in the Parseval frame
\text{\large{$\boldsymbol{\Psi}$}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
, the inclusion
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathfrak{B}}\subset\mathfrak{F}^{\infty}
and the Cotlar-Stein procedure one concludes from the Proposition
3.8
that:

Corollary 3.9
.

P_{\mathfrak{B}}
has an integral kernel
\mathfrak{K}(P_{\mathfrak{B}})\in\mathring{\mathscr{S}}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X})
(see Notation
2.1
) given by the series:

\mathfrak{K}(P_{\mathfrak{B}})(x,y):=\underset{\gamma\in\Gamma}{\sum}\,\underset{p\in\underline{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}}{\sum}(\tau_{-\gamma}\psi_{p})(x)\,\overline{(\tau_{-\gamma}\psi_{p})(y)}\,,

with uniform convergence on compact sets in
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
. Moreover, the limit in Proposition
C.3
exists in the strong operator topology on
L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
.

We notice that the map
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathfrak{B}}
appearing in the above proof is in fact the coordinate map associated with the Parseval frame
\text{\large{$\boldsymbol{\Psi}$}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
, as in Remark
C.5
:

\mathfrak{C}_{\mathfrak{B}}:P_{\mathfrak{B}}\,L^{2}(\mathcal{X})\ni\,f\,\mapsto\,\big((\tau_{\alpha}\psi_{p}\,,\,f)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}\big)_{(\alpha,p)\in\Gamma\times\underline{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}}\,\in\,[\ell^{2}(\Gamma)]^{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}\cong\ell^{2}(\Gamma)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}.

Corollary 3.10
.

The map
\mathfrak{W}_{\mathfrak{B}}:\mathbb{B}\big(P_{\mathfrak{B}}L^{2}(\mathcal{X})\big)\ni\,T\mapsto\mathfrak{C}_{\mathfrak{B}}\,T\,\mathfrak{C}_{\mathfrak{B}}^{*}\in\mathbb{B}\big(\ell^{2}(\Gamma)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}\big)
is a
*
-homomorphism of
C^{*}
-algebras (in general not surjective).

Notation 3.11
.

Given any
T\in\mathbb{B}\big(P_{\mathfrak{B}}L^{2}(\mathcal{X})\big)
and the canonical basis
\big\{{{\cal{e}}_{\alpha}\otimes{\cal{e}}_{p}}\big\}_{(\alpha,p)\in\Gamma\times\underline{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}}
of
\ell^{2}(\Gamma)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}
we shall use the notation
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathfrak{B}}[T]\in\mathscr{M}^{\circ}_{\Gamma}[\mathscr{M}_{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}]
for the infinite matrix:

\big[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathfrak{B}}[T]_{\alpha,\beta}\big]_{p,q}\,:=\,\big({\cal{e}}_{\alpha}\otimes{\cal{e}}_{p}\,,\,\mathfrak{W}_{\mathfrak{B}}[T]\,{\cal{e}}_{\beta}\otimes{\cal{e}}_{q}\big)_{\ell^{2}(\Gamma)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}}\,=\,\big((\tau_{-\alpha}\psi_{p})\,,\,T\,(\tau_{-\beta}\psi_{q})\big)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}.

(3.25)

When the operator
T\in\mathbb{B}\big(P_{\mathfrak{B}}L^{2}(\mathcal{X})\big)
has a distribution kernel
\mathfrak{K}(T)\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X})
, we can write:

\big[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathfrak{B}}[T]_{\alpha,\beta}\big]_{p,q}=\big((\tau_{-\alpha}\psi_{p})\,,\,(\mathfrak{I}\mathit{nt}\,\mathfrak{K}(T))\,(\tau_{-\beta}\psi_{q})\big)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}=\big\langle\,\mathfrak{K}(T)\,,\,\overline{(\tau_{-\alpha}\psi_{p})}\otimes(\tau_{-\beta}\psi_{q})\,\big\rangle_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}}.

When
T
commutes with the translations with vectors from
\Gamma
, its distribution kernel satisfies the identities:

(\tau_{\gamma}\otimes\tau_{\gamma})\mathfrak{K}(T)=\mathfrak{K}(T)\mbox{ for any }\gamma\in\Gamma\,,

and thus the following equalities prove that there exists
\mathring{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathfrak{B}}[T]\in{\cal{s}}(\Gamma;\mathscr{M}_{n_{\mathfrak{B}}})
such that:

\displaystyle\big[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathfrak{B}}[T]_{\alpha,\beta}\big]_{p,q}

\displaystyle=\big((\tau_{-\alpha}\psi_{p})\,,\,(\mathfrak{I}\mathit{nt}\,\mathfrak{K}(T))\,(\tau_{-\beta}\psi_{q})\big)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}

\displaystyle=\big(\psi_{p}\,,\,\mathfrak{I}\mathit{nt}\big[(\tau_{\alpha-\beta}\otimes{\rm{1}\hskip-3.0pt\mathbf{l}})\mathfrak{K}(T)\big]\psi_{q}\big)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}=:\big[\mathring{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathfrak{B}}[T]_{\alpha-\beta}\big]_{p,q}.

Taking now
T=H_{\mathfrak{B}}\in\mathbb{B}\big(P_{\mathfrak{B}}L^{2}(\mathcal{X})\big)
we obtain the following infinite matrix with rapid off-diagonal decay:

\displaystyle\big[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathfrak{B}}[H_{\mathfrak{B}}]_{\alpha,\beta}\big]_{p,q}=\big((\tau_{-\alpha}\psi_{p})\,,\,\big(\mathfrak{I}\mathit{nt}\,\mathfrak{K}(\widehat{H}_{\mathfrak{B}})\big)\,(\tau_{-\beta}\psi_{q})\big)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}

(3.26)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}_{*}}d\theta\,e^{i<\theta,\alpha-\beta>}\,\Big(\underset{k_{0}\leq k\leq k_{0}+N}{\sum}\lambda_{k}(\theta)\big(\hat{\psi}_{q}(\theta),\hat{\pi}_{k}(\theta)\hat{\psi}_{p}(\theta)\big)_{\mathscr{F}_{\theta}}\Big)\,,

and the following rapidly decaying sequence (as stated in point 3.
3b
in Theorem
2.18
):

\displaystyle\big[[\mathring{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\widehat{H}_{\mathfrak{B}}]_{\gamma}\big]_{p,q}\,

\displaystyle=\,\int_{\mathbb{T}_{*}}d\theta\,e^{i<\theta,\gamma>}\,\Big(\underset{k_{0}\leq k\leq k_{0}+N}{\sum}\lambda_{k}(\theta)\big(\hat{\psi}_{q}(\theta),\hat{\pi}_{k}(\theta)\hat{\psi}_{p}(\theta)\big)_{\mathscr{F}_{\theta}}\Big)

(3.27)

\displaystyle=\,\int_{\mathbb{T}_{*}}d\theta\,e^{i<\theta,\gamma>}\,\big(\hat{\psi}_{q}(\theta),\widehat{H}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\theta)\hat{\psi}_{p}(\theta)\big)_{\mathscr{F}_{\theta}}.

If we denote by
\mathring{U}(\gamma)
the restriction to
\ell^{2}(\Gamma)
of the translation with
-\gamma\in\Gamma
and by:
