, and we fix two cut-off functions
\varphi
and
\widetilde{\varphi}
in
C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
that are equal to
1
on
J
, have their support included in
J^{\delta}_{\mathfrak{B}}
and verify the equality
\varphi=\widetilde{\varphi}\,\varphi
.
For any
v\in E^{\epsilon}_{h}(J)\mathcal{H}
we may write:

e^{-itH^{\epsilon}}v=E^{\epsilon}_{h}(J)e^{-itH^{\epsilon}}{\varphi}\big(H^{\epsilon}\big)E^{\epsilon}_{h}(J)v=\widetilde{\varphi}(H^{\epsilon})e^{-itH^{\epsilon}}{\varphi}\big(H^{\epsilon}\big)\widetilde{\varphi}(H^{\epsilon})v.

For any
t\in\mathbb{R}
let us define
\varphi_{t}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
by
\varphi_{t}(s):=e^{-its}\varphi(s)
, so that the above equality becomes:

e^{-itH^{\epsilon}}v=\widetilde{\varphi}(H^{\epsilon})\varphi_{t}\big(H^{\epsilon}\big)\widetilde{\varphi}(H^{\epsilon})v.

(5.5)

We use now the Helffer-Sjöstrand formula (see
[
35
,
27
]
).
For that we fix an auxiliary cut-off function
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R};[0,1])
having support in
\{|t|\leq 2\}
and being equal to 1 on
\{|t|\leq 1\}
and define (notice a slight difference with
[
27
]
in the choice of such a quasi-analytic extension):

\overset{\frown}{\varphi}_{t,N}:{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\quad\overset{\frown}{\varphi}_{t,N}(x+iy):=\underset{0\leq k\leq N}{\sum}(\partial^{k}\varphi_{t})(x)(iy)^{k}(k!)^{-1}\chi(y).

Then for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the support of
\overset{\frown}{\varphi}_{t,N}
is a compact set contained in
\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits\varphi_{t}\times[-2,2]
and
\overset{\frown}{\varphi}_{t,N}
is smooth. Moreover:

\begin{split}\frac{\partial\overset{\frown}{\varphi}_{t,N}}{\partial\overline{\mathfrak{z}}}(\mathfrak{z})&=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\partial\overset{\frown}{\varphi}_{t,N}}{\partial x}+i\frac{\partial\overset{\frown}{\varphi}_{t,N}}{\partial y}\right)\\
&\hskip-56.9055pt=\frac{1}{2}\Big[i\underset{0\leq k\leq N}{\sum}(\partial^{k}\varphi_{t})(x)(iy)^{k}(k!)^{-1}[(\partial\chi)(y)]+(\partial^{N+1}\varphi_{t})(x)(iy)^{N}(N!)^{-1}\chi(y)\Big]\,,\end{split}

(5.6)

and we see that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
:

\underset{y\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\,\,\,|y|^{-N}\,\big|(\partial_{\overline{\mathcal{z}}}\overset{\frown}{\varphi}_{t,N})(x+iy)\big|=\big|(\partial^{N+1}\varphi_{t})(x)(N!)^{-1}\big|<\infty.

We notice the important fact that
(\partial^{k}\varphi_{t})(x)
is a polynomial of degree
k
in
t\in\mathbb{R}
whose coefficients are smooth complex functions of
x\in\mathbb{R}
having all their support contained in
\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits(\varphi)
. As functions of
t
, these terms can grow at most like
<t>^{N}
.

Let us use (
5.5
) and Proposition
4.13
in order to get:

\begin{array}[]{ll}e^{-itH^{\epsilon}}v&=\widetilde{\varphi}(H^{\epsilon})\varphi_{t}\big(H^{\epsilon}\big)\widetilde{\varphi}(H^{\epsilon})v=P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\,\widetilde{\varphi}(H^{\epsilon})\varphi_{t}\big(H^{\epsilon}\big)\widetilde{\varphi}(H^{\epsilon})\,P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\,v\,+\,\epsilon\,X_{\epsilon}v\\
&=P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}{\varphi}_{t}\big(H^{\epsilon}\big)P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}v\,+\,\epsilon\,X_{\epsilon}v\\
&={\pi^{-1}}\iint\,\big(-\frac{i}{2}d\mathcal{z}d\bar{\mathcal{z}}\big)\,\big(\partial_{\overline{\mathcal{z}}}\overset{\frown}{\varphi}_{t,N}\big)(\mathcal{z},\overline{\mathcal{z}})\,P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\big(H^{\epsilon}-\mathcal{z}{\rm{1}\hskip-3.0pt\mathbf{l}}\big)^{-1}P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}v\,+\,\epsilon\,X_{\epsilon}v\\
&={\pi^{-1}}\iint\,\big(-\frac{i}{2}d\mathcal{z}d\bar{\mathcal{z}}\big)\,\big(\partial_{\overline{\mathcal{z}}}\overset{\frown}{\varphi}_{t,N}\big)(\mathcal{z},\overline{\mathcal{z}})\,P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}R^{\sim}_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{z})P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}v\,+\,\epsilon\,X_{\epsilon}v\end{array}

with
\|X_{\epsilon}\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2}(\mathcal{X}))}\leq 1
uniformly for
\epsilon\in[0,\epsilon_{0}]
.
For
\epsilon
small enough we also have the identity

e^{-it\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}}v=e^{-it\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}}P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\,\widetilde{\varphi}(H^{\epsilon})\,v\,+\,\mathscr{O}(\epsilon)\,v=\varphi_{t}\big(\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\big)P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\,v\,+\,\mathscr{O}(\epsilon)\,v.

Hence we may compute:

\displaystyle\big\|e^{-itH^{\epsilon}}v\,-\,e^{-it\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}}v\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}\,

\displaystyle{=}\,\big\|P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}{\varphi}_{t}\big(H^{\epsilon}\big)P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}v\,-\,P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\varphi_{t}\big(\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\big)P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}v\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}\,+\,\mathscr{O}(\epsilon)\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}

\displaystyle\hskip-142.26378pt\leq\iint\big(\frac{d\mathcal{z}d\bar{\mathcal{z}}}{2\pi}\big)\,\big|\big(\partial_{\overline{\mathcal{z}}}\overset{\frown}{\varphi}_{t,N}\big)(\mathcal{z},\overline{\mathcal{z}})\big|\,\Big\|P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\Big[R^{\epsilon}(\mathcal{z})\,-\,\big(\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}-\mathcal{z}P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\big)^{-1}\Big]\,P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}v\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}+\,\mathscr{O}(\epsilon)\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}.

Using Theorem
2.18
and Remark
5.2
, we notice the identity
P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}R^{\epsilon}(\mathcal{z})P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}=P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}R^{\sim}_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{z})P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}
and the estimate:

\displaystyle\Big\|P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\Big[R^{\sim}_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{z})\,-\,\big(\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}-\mathcal{z}P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\big)^{-1}\Big]\,P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}

\displaystyle\leq\Big\|P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}H^{\epsilon}[R^{\bot}_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{z})]H^{\epsilon}P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}\leq C(\delta)\,(\Im\hskip-1.0pt{\cal{m}}\mathcal{z})^{-2}\,\epsilon^{2}\,\big\|R^{\bot}_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{z})\big\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2}(\mathcal{X}))}.

Finally, by taking
N=2
in the definition of
\overset{\frown}{\varphi}_{t,N}
and using (
5.6
), we have the bound:

\displaystyle\iint\,\big(-\frac{i}{2}d\mathcal{z}d\bar{\mathcal{z}}\big)\,\big|\big(\partial_{\overline{\mathcal{z}}}\overset{\frown}{\varphi}_{t,2}\big)(\mathcal{z},\overline{\mathcal{z}})\big|\,|\Im\hskip-1.0pt{\cal{m}}\mathcal{z}|^{-2}=\iint_{\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits(\widetilde{\varphi}_{t,2})}\,dx\,dy\,\big|\big(\partial_{\overline{\mathcal{z}}}\overset{\frown}{\varphi}_{t,2}\big)(x+iy)\big|\,|y|^{-2}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leq\int_{\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits(\varphi)}\,dx\,\left[\underset{0\leq k\leq 2}{\sum}\big[\underset{x\in\mathbb{R}}{\sup}\big|\big(\partial^{k}\varphi_{t}\big)(x)\big|\big]\int_{1}^{2}\,dy\,y^{k-2}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\int_{\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits(\varphi)}\,dx\,\big[\underset{x\in\mathbb{R}}{\sup}\big|\big(\partial^{3}\varphi_{t}\big)(x)\big|\big]\int_{0}^{2}\,dy.

One concludes that there exist
C>0
and
\epsilon_{0}>0
such that for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
and for any
\epsilon\in[0,\epsilon_{0}]
we have the estimate:

\displaystyle\big\|e^{-itH^{\epsilon}}v\,-\,e^{-it\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}}v\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}\,

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\hskip-113.81102pt\leq\,\Big[C\,\epsilon^{2}\,<t>^{3}\big(\hskip-8.5359pt\underset{{\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{c}\Re\hskip-1.0pt\mathcal{e}\mathcal{z}\in\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits(\varphi),\\
|\Im\hskip-1.0pt{\cal{m}}\mathcal{z}|\leq 2\end{array}}}{\sup}\hskip-8.5359pt\big\|[R^{\bot}_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{z})]\big\|_{\mathbb{B}(L^{2}(\mathcal{X}))}\big)\,+\,\mathscr{O}(\epsilon)\Big]\,\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}\,,\quad\forall v\in E^{\epsilon}_{h}(J)\mathcal{H}.

∎

6
Magnetic fields having a non-zero constant component.

We dedicate this last section to a more refined analysis of Formula (
4.33
) in order to obtain a better understanding of the ’singular term’
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\Gamma}^{\epsilon}\mathfrak{M}_{\mathfrak{B}}[H_{\mathfrak{B}}]
and of the first order correction in (
4.33
). For that we shall consider that our perturbing magnetic field is a small perturbation of a weak constant magnetic field
B^{\bullet}:=dA^{\bullet}
.

We shall consider a magnetic field
B(x)=\epsilon\big(B^{\bullet}+\mathcal{c}{B}^{\epsilon}(x)\big)
as in Hypothesis
2.20
. The vector potential defining
B(x)
will be taken of the form
A(x)\equiv\epsilon\big(A^{\bullet}(x)+\mathcal{c}\,A^{\epsilon}(x)\big)=\epsilon{A}^{\mathcal{c}}(x)
.

6.1
The constant perturbing magnetic field.

In this subsection we work under Hypothesis
2.20
with the parameter
\mathcal{c}=0
, i.e. with a constant magnetic field of the form
B=\epsilon{B}^{\bullet}
. We keep using the notations (
2.11
) but the perturbing magnetic field is now constant. Thus the entire analysis and all the results in the previous section are true, but we shall put into evidence some important specific features valid in this particular case.

The main new feature when dealing with a constant magnetic field comes from the Zak translations (
4.4
).

6.1.1
The Zak translations in constant magnetic field

Definition 6.1
.

Given a constant magnetic field of the form
\epsilon\,B^{\bullet}
, we call
Zak translations
with vectors from
\Gamma
the following family of twisted unitary translations on
L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
(using the notation in (
2.11
))

{\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\gamma}f(x)}:=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}(x,\gamma)\,f(x-\gamma)=\Lambda^{\epsilon}_{\gamma}(x)\,f(x-\gamma),\quad\ \forall\gamma\in\Gamma.

(6.1)

For our constant magnetic field situation, we recall the following results in
[
18
]
:

Proposition 6.2
.

The family in (
6.1
) satisfies the following properties:

1.

The map
\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}:\Gamma\ni\gamma\rightarrow\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\gamma}\in\mathbb{U}\big(L^{2}(\mathcal{X})\big)
defines a projective representation with 2-cocycle
[\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}]^{-1}:\Gamma\times\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}
i.e.

\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\alpha}\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\beta}=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}(\beta,\alpha)\,\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\alpha+\beta}\,.

2.

The operator
\mathfrak{Op}^{\epsilon}(F)
commutes with all the
\{\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
if and only if
F\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\Xi)
is
\Gamma
-periodic with respect to the variable in
\mathcal{X}
.

6.1.2
The magnetic isolated Bloch family in constant magnetic field.

The essential consequence following from point 1 in Proposition
6.2
above is that we have the commutation relation:

\forall(\alpha,\gamma)\in\Gamma\times\Gamma:\quad\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\alpha}\,\big[\mathfrak{I}\mathit{nt}\,\mathfrak{K}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B},\gamma}\big]\,=\,\big[\mathfrak{I}\mathit{nt}\,\mathfrak{K}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B},\gamma}\big]\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\alpha}

implying that
\widetilde{Q}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}
and all its assoociated functional calculus are in the commutant of the family
\{\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
. It follows that
\Theta^{\epsilon}
and also
P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}
commute with the family
\{\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
and we evidently have the following statement true.

Proposition 6.3
.

In the case of a constant perturbing magnetic field
B:=\epsilon{B}^{\bullet}
, the space
\mathfrak{L}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is equal to the closure of the linear span over
{\mathbb{C}}
of the family
\{\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\gamma}\psi^{\epsilon}_{p}\}_{(\gamma,p)\in\Gamma\times n_{\mathfrak{B}}}
with:

\psi^{\epsilon}_{p}\,=\,\Theta^{\epsilon}\psi_{p}\,,\quad\forall{p}\in n_{\mathfrak{B}}\,,

(6.2)

and

P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\,=\,\mathfrak{I}\mathit{nt}\Big[\underset{(p,\gamma)\in n_{\mathfrak{B}}\times\Gamma}{\sum}\big(\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\gamma}\psi^{\epsilon}_{p}\big)\otimes\big(\overline{\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\gamma}\psi^{\epsilon}_{p}}\big)\Big].

(6.3)

Corollary 6.4
.

The family
\text{\large{$\boldsymbol{\Psi}$}}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}:=\big\{\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\alpha}\psi^{\epsilon}_{p}\ \forall(\alpha,p)\in\Gamma\times\underline{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}\big\}
is a strongly localized Parseval frame for the closed subspace
\mathcal{L}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}
that it generates in
L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
.

Notice that this is the equivalent of (
4.23
) for the situation when the perturbing magnetic field is constant.

We end this subsection with the following technical result about the family of functions
\{\psi^{\epsilon}_{p}\}_{p\in n_{\mathfrak{B}}}
introduced in (
6.2
).

Lemma 6.5
.

There exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
such that for any
N>0
there exists
C_{N}>0
such that for all
(\alpha,p)\in\Gamma\times\underline{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}
and
\epsilon\in[0,\epsilon_{0}]
, if we denote by
Q
the operator of multiplication with the variable
x\in\mathcal{X}
in
L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
, we have the estimations

\big\|<Q-\alpha>^{N}\Big(\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\alpha}\psi^{\epsilon}_{p}-\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\alpha}\psi_{p}\Big)\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}\leq C_{N}\,\epsilon\,\|<Q-\alpha>^{N+1}\psi_{p}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}.

Proof.

Because
\Theta^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}
commutes with the Zak translations, it is enough to prove this for
\alpha=0
. Since
P_{\mathfrak{B}}\psi_{p}=\psi_{p}
, using (
4.17
) we may write

\displaystyle\Theta^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\,\psi_{p}-\psi_{p}

\displaystyle=-(2\pi i)^{-1}\oint_{\mathscr{C}}d\mathcal{z}\,\mathcal{z}^{-1/2}\,\Big(\big(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathfrak{B}}^{\epsilon}-\mathcal{z}{\rm{1}\hskip-3.0pt\mathbf{l}}\big)^{-1}-\big(P_{\mathfrak{B}}-\mathcal{z}{\rm{1}\hskip-3.0pt\mathbf{l}}\big)^{-1}\Big)\,\psi_{p}

(6.4)

\displaystyle=(2\pi i)^{-1}\oint_{\mathscr{C}}d\mathcal{z}\,\mathcal{z}^{-1/2}\,\big(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathfrak{B}}^{\epsilon}-\mathcal{z}{\rm{1}\hskip-3.0pt\mathbf{l}}\big)^{-1}\Big(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathfrak{B}}^{\epsilon}-P_{\mathfrak{B}}\Big)\,\big(P_{\mathfrak{B}}-\mathcal{z}{\rm{1}\hskip-3.0pt\mathbf{l}}\big)^{-1}\,\psi_{p}.

Using Lemma
4.11
, Remark
4.10
and the fact that
|\Lambda^{\epsilon}(x,y)-1|\leq C\,\epsilon
, we obtain that, for any
N\geq 1
, there exists
C_{N}
and
\epsilon_{0}>0
such that, for any
f
with rapid decay and any
\epsilon\in[0,\epsilon_{0}]
, we have

\|<Q>^{N}\,\big(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathfrak{B}}^{\epsilon}-P_{\mathfrak{B}}\big)f\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}\leq C_{N}\,\epsilon\,\|<Q>^{N+1}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}.

Also, denoting by
X
either
P_{\mathfrak{B}}
or
\widetilde{Q}_{\mathfrak{B}}^{\epsilon}
we have, for any
N\geq 1
,

\sup_{\mathcal{z}\in\mathscr{C}}\Big\|<\cdot>^{N}\big(X-\mathcal{z}{\rm{1}\hskip-3.0pt\mathbf{l}}\big)^{-1}\,<\cdot>^{-N}\Big\|=C^{\prime}_{N}<\infty,

which may be proved by showing that all possible multiple commutators between the position operator and the resolvent can be extended to bounded operators. Implementing all this in (
6.4
) the proof is finished.
∎

6.1.3
The magnetic infinite matrices in a constant perturbing magnetic field

We shall put now into evidence the important feature characterizing the ’effective magnetic Hamiltonian’ for the isolated Bloch family in a constant magnetic perturbing field.

For a linear operator
T\in\,\mathbb{B}\big(P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\,L^{2}(\mathcal{X})\big)
we use the notation

\mathfrak{M}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}[T]\,:=\,\big(\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\alpha}\,\psi^{\epsilon}_{p}\,,\,T\,\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\beta}\,\psi^{\epsilon}_{q}\big)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}

(6.5)

for its infinite matrix defined by the Parseval frame in Corollary
6.4
and notice that this is simply the matrix in (
4.24
) for the situation when the perturbing magnetic field is constant. The commutation properties discussed in this section for the case of a constant perturbing magnetic field have the following interesting consequence concerning the structure of the matrices
\mathfrak{M}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\mathfrak{Op}^{\epsilon}(F)]
for a
\Gamma
-periodic symbol
F
.

Proposition 6.6
.

Given a constant magnetic field
B:=\epsilon{B}^{\bullet}=\epsilon\,dA^{\bullet}
and a magnetic operator
\mathfrak{I}\mathit{nt}\,\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}\mathfrak{K}
with
\mathfrak{K}\in\mathring{\mathscr{S}}_{\Delta}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X})
such that
(\tau_{\gamma}\otimes\tau_{\gamma})\mathfrak{K}=\mathfrak{K}
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
, there exists
\mathring{\mathfrak{m}}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\mathfrak{K}]\in{\cal{s}}(\Gamma;\mathscr{M}_{n_{\mathfrak{B}}})
such that:

\mathfrak{M}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\mathfrak{I}\mathit{nt}\,\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}\mathfrak{K}]_{\alpha,\beta}\,=\,\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}(\alpha,\beta)\,\mathring{\mathfrak{m}}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\mathfrak{K}]_{\alpha-\beta}\,.

Proof.

Taking into account Proposition
6.2
, let us compute:

\displaystyle\big[\mathfrak{M}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\mathfrak{I}\mathit{nt}\,\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}\mathfrak{K}]_{\alpha,\beta}\big]_{p,q}

\displaystyle=\big(\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\alpha}\,\psi^{\epsilon}_{p}\,,\,[\mathfrak{I}\mathit{nt}\,\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}\,\mathfrak{K}[F]]\,\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\beta}\,\psi^{\epsilon}_{q}\big)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}(\alpha,\beta)\big(\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\alpha-\beta}\,\psi^{\epsilon}_{p}\,,\,[\mathfrak{I}\mathit{nt}\,\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}\,\mathfrak{K}[F]]\,\psi^{\epsilon}_{q}\big)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}

and identify:

\big[\mathring{\mathfrak{m}}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\mathfrak{K}]_{\gamma}\big]_{p,q}\,:=\,\big(\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\gamma}\,\psi^{\epsilon}_{p}\,,\,[\mathfrak{I}\mathit{nt}\,\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}\,\mathfrak{K}(][F]]\,\psi^{\epsilon}_{q}\big)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}.

∎

Definition 6.7
.

For any
\epsilon\in[0,1]
we denote by
\vec{\mathscr{M}}^{\epsilon}_{\Gamma}[\mathscr{M}_{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}]
the complex linear space of the matrices
\mathfrak{M}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}
in
\mathscr{M}^{\circ}_{\Gamma}[\mathscr{M}_{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}]
satisfying the following property (as in Proposition
6.6
):

\exists\,\mathring{\mathfrak{m}}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\in{\cal{s}}(\Gamma;\mathscr{M}_{n_{\mathfrak{B}}})\ \text{such that}\ [\mathfrak{M}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}]_{\alpha,\beta}\,=\,\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}(\alpha,\beta)\,[\mathring{\mathfrak{m}}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}]_{\alpha-\beta}.

Proposition 6.8
.

The space
\vec{\mathscr{M}}^{\epsilon}_{\Gamma}[\mathscr{M}_{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}]
is a normed subalgebra of
\mathring{\mathscr{M}}_{\Gamma}[\mathscr{M}_{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}]
with involution and unity.

Proof.

We reproduce here the proof of (3.28) in
[
18
]
.
Given
(S,T)\in\big[\mathscr{M}^{\circ}_{\Gamma}[\mathscr{M}_{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}]_{\epsilon}\big]^{2}
we know that

S_{\alpha,\beta}=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}(\alpha,\beta)\mathring{S}_{\alpha-\beta}\mbox{ and }T_{\alpha,\beta}=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}(\alpha,\beta)\mathring{T}_{\alpha-\beta}\mbox{ with }(\mathring{S},\mathring{T})\in\big[{\cal{s}}\big(\Gamma;\mathscr{M}_{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}\big)\big]^{2}\,.

Hence

\displaystyle(ST)_{\alpha,\beta}:

\displaystyle=\underset{\gamma\in\Gamma}{\sum}S_{\alpha,\gamma}\,T_{\gamma,\beta}=\underset{\gamma\in\Gamma}{\sum}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}(\alpha,\gamma)\mathring{S}_{\gamma-\alpha}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}(\gamma,\beta)\mathring{T}_{\beta-\gamma}=\underset{\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma}{\sum}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}(\alpha,\gamma^{\prime})\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}(\gamma^{\prime}+\alpha,\beta)\mathring{S}_{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathring{T}_{\beta-\alpha-\gamma^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}(\alpha,\beta)\underset{\gamma\in\Gamma}{\sum}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}(\alpha,\gamma)\Lambda^{\epsilon}(\gamma,\beta)\mathring{S}_{\gamma}\mathring{T}_{\beta-\alpha-\gamma}=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}(\alpha,\beta)\underset{\gamma\in\Gamma}{\sum}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}(\gamma,\beta-\alpha)\mathring{S}_{\gamma}\mathring{T}_{\beta-\alpha-\gamma}\,,

and we can define
\mathring{ST}\in{\cal{s}}\big(\Gamma;\mathscr{M}_{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}\big)
by the formula:

[\mathring{ST}]_{\alpha}:=\underset{\gamma\in\Gamma}{\sum}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}(\gamma,\alpha)\,\mathring{S}_{\gamma}\cdot\mathring{T}_{\alpha-\gamma}\,.

∎

Proposition 6.9
.

There exists some
\epsilon_{0}>0
and for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists some
C_{n}>0
such that:

<\gamma>^{n}\big|\big[\mathring{\mathfrak{m}}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\widehat{H}_{\mathfrak{B}}]\big]_{\gamma}-\big[\mathring{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\widehat{H}_{\mathfrak{B}}]_{\gamma}\big]_{p,q}\big|\,\leq\,C_{n}\,\epsilon\,,\quad\forall\epsilon\in[0,\epsilon_{0}]\,.

(6.6)

Proof.

Starting from Definition
6.7
and (
3.26
)–(
3.27
) we can write:

\big[\mathring{\mathfrak{m}}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\widehat{H}_{\mathfrak{B}}]_{\gamma}\big]_{(p,q)}=\big(\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\gamma}\,\psi^{\epsilon}_{p}\,,\,\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\,\psi^{\epsilon}_{q}\big)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}=\big(\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\gamma}\,\psi^{\epsilon}_{p}\,,\,H^{\epsilon}\,\psi^{\epsilon}_{q}\big)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})},

(6.7)

and

\big[\mathring{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\widehat{H}_{\mathfrak{B}}]_{\gamma}\big]_{(p,q)}=\mathfrak{M}_{\mathfrak{B}}[H_{\mathfrak{B}}]_{(\gamma,p),(0,q)}=\big(\tau_{-\gamma}\,\psi_{p}\,,\,H_{\mathfrak{B}}\,\psi_{q}\big)_{L^{2}(X)}\,.

(6.8)

Then we have to estimate:

\displaystyle<\gamma>^{n}\big|\big[\mathring{\mathfrak{m}}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\widehat{H}_{\mathfrak{B}}]\big]_{\gamma}-\big[\mathring{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\widehat{H}_{\mathfrak{B}}]_{\gamma}\big]_{p,q}\big|=<\gamma>^{n}\big|\big(\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\gamma}\,\psi_{p}^{\epsilon}\,,\,H^{\epsilon}\,\psi^{\epsilon}_{q}\big)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}-\big(\tau_{-\gamma}\psi_{p}\,,\,H_{\mathfrak{B}}\,\psi_{q}\big)_{L^{2}(X)}\big|

\displaystyle\hskip 12.0pt\leq\,<\gamma>^{n}\big|\big(\Lambda^{\epsilon}_{\gamma}\,\tau_{-\gamma}\Theta^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\,\psi_{p}\,,\,\mathfrak{Op}^{\epsilon}(p^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\sharp^{\epsilon}\,h\sharp^{\epsilon}\,p^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}-h_{\mathfrak{B}})\,\Theta^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\,\psi_{q}\big)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}\big|+

\displaystyle\hskip 24.0pt+<\gamma>^{n}\,\big|\big(\Lambda^{\epsilon}_{\gamma}\,\tau_{-\gamma}\Theta^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\,\psi_{p}\,,\,\mathfrak{Op}^{\epsilon}(h_{\mathfrak{B}})\,\Theta^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\,\psi_{q}\big)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}-\big(\tau_{-\gamma}\psi_{p}\,,\,\mathfrak{Op}^{A^{\circ}}(h_{\mathfrak{B}})\,\psi_{q}\big)_{L^{2}(X)}\big|.

First, we notice that Proposition
B.12
and Remark
4.12
imply that
p^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\sharp^{\epsilon}\,h\sharp^{\epsilon}\,p^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}-h_{\mathfrak{B}}=\mathscr{O}(\epsilon)
as symbols of bounded operators and the rapid decay of the functions
\psi_{p}
for
p\in\underline{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}
allows to control the factor
<\gamma>^{n}
by the decay of
\tau_{-\gamma}\psi_{p}
. In order to estimate
\Lambda^{\epsilon}_{\gamma}\,\tau_{-\gamma}\Theta^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}\,\psi_{p}
we use Lemma
6.5
. Thus we obtain the estimate:

\displaystyle<\gamma>^{n}

\displaystyle\big|\big[\mathring{\mathfrak{m}}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\widehat{H}_{\mathfrak{B}}]\big]_{\gamma}-\big[\mathring{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\widehat{H}_{\mathfrak{B}}]_{\gamma}\big]_{p,q}\big|=<\gamma>^{n}\big(\tau_{-\gamma}\psi_{p}\,,\,\mathfrak{I}\mathit{nt}\big[\big(\Lambda^{\epsilon}_{\gamma}\,\Lambda^{\epsilon}-1\big)\mathfrak{K}^{\circ}(h_{\mathfrak{B}})\big]\,\psi_{q}\big)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}\hskip-5.69046pt+\mathscr{O}(\epsilon).

In order to finish the proof we notice that:

\displaystyle<\gamma>^{n}

\displaystyle\big(\tau_{-\gamma}\psi_{p}\,,\,\mathfrak{I}\mathit{nt}\big[\big(\Lambda^{\epsilon}_{\gamma}\,\Lambda^{\epsilon}-1\big)\mathfrak{K}^{\circ}(h_{\mathfrak{B}})\big]\,\psi_{q}\big)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}=

\displaystyle=<\gamma>^{n}\int_{\mathcal{X}}dx\int_{\mathcal{X}}dy\,\overline{\psi_{p}(x-\gamma)}\,\big(i\epsilon\langle\,B^{\bullet},(\gamma\wedge x+y\wedge x)\rangle\big)\,\times

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt\times\,\Big(\int_{0}^{1}ds\,\exp\big(-is\epsilon\langle\,B^{\bullet},x\wedge\gamma+x\wedge y\rangle\big)\Big)\,\mathfrak{K}^{\circ}(h_{\mathfrak{B}})(x,y)\,\psi_{q}(y).

As
\gamma\wedge x=(\gamma-x)\wedge x
and
y\wedge x=y\wedge(x-y)
we obtain that, for any
M_{1},M_{2}
and
M_{3}
in
\mathbb{N}
, we can write:

\begin{split}\Big|<\gamma>^{n}&\big(\tau_{-\gamma}\psi_{p}\,,\,\mathfrak{I}\mathit{nt}\big[\big(\Lambda^{\epsilon}_{\gamma}\,\Lambda^{\epsilon}-1\big)\mathfrak{K}^{\circ}(h_{\mathfrak{B}})\big]\,\psi_{q}\big)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}\Big|\leq\,C\,\epsilon\int_{\mathcal{X}}dx\int_{\mathcal{X}}dy\,\times\\
&\hskip 12.0pt\times\,<x-\gamma>^{M_{1}}|\psi_{p}(x-\gamma)|\,<x-y>^{M_{2}}|\mathfrak{K}^{\circ}(h_{\mathfrak{B}})(x,y)|\,<y>^{M_{3}}|\psi_{q}(x)|\,\times\\
&\hskip 12.0pt\times\,<\gamma>^{n}<x-\gamma>^{-M_{1}}\big(<x-\gamma><x>+<x-y><y>\big)<x-y>^{-M_{2}}<y>^{-M_{3}}.\end{split}

(6.9)

Finally we notice that
<\gamma>^{n}\leq\,C_{n}<\mathbf{x}-\gamma>^{n}<x>^{n}
and
<x>^{n+1}\leq\,C^{\prime}_{n}<x-y>^{n+1}<y>^{n+1}
and we only have to choose
M_{1}\geq n+1
,
M_{2}\geq n+2
and
M_{3}\geq n+2
in order to obtain a bound of order
\epsilon
for the above scalar product and finish the proof.
∎

6.2
Weak fluctuations on a constant perturbing magnetic field.

It is evident that Hypothesis
2.20
implies Hypothesis
2.10
and thus all the results obtained in Sections
4
and
5
in particular Theorem
2.18
remain true, but we shall reformulate them in a way that puts into evidence the presence of the non-vanishing constant part of the perturbing magnetic field.

Working under Hypothesis
2.20
on the perturbing magnetic field, the total magnetic field for the magnetic pseudo-differential calculus is
B^{\circ}+\epsilon\big(B^{\bullet}+\mathcal{c}\,B^{\epsilon}\big)
and the notations introduced in (
2.11
) have to be modified into:

\begin{split}&\Lambda^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}\,\equiv\,\Lambda^{A^{\circ}+\epsilon\,A^{\mathcal{c}}},\ \Lambda^{\epsilon}\,\equiv\,\Lambda^{A^{\circ}+\epsilon{A}^{\bullet}},\ \widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}\equiv\Lambda^{\mathcal{c}\epsilon{A}^{\epsilon}},\ \widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}\equiv\Lambda^{\epsilon{A}^{\bullet}}\\
&\mathfrak{Op}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}\,\equiv\,\mathfrak{Op}^{A^{\circ}+\epsilon{A}^{\mathcal{c}}},\ \mathfrak{Op}^{\epsilon}\equiv\mathfrak{Op}^{A^{\circ}+\epsilon{A}^{\bullet}}\\
&\Omega^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}\,\equiv\,\Omega^{B^{\circ}+\epsilon\,B^{\mathcal{c}}},\ \Omega^{\epsilon}\,\equiv\,\Omega^{B^{\circ}+\epsilon{B}^{\bullet}},\ \widetilde{\Omega}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}\equiv\Omega^{\mathcal{c}\epsilon{B}^{\epsilon}},\\
&\sharp^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}\,\equiv\,\sharp^{B^{\circ}+\epsilon{B}^{\mathcal{c}}},\ \sharp^{\epsilon}\,\equiv\,\sharp^{B^{\circ}+\epsilon{B}^{\bullet}}.\end{split}

(6.10)

In agreement with our new notations (
6.10
) we shall modify the main notations from Sections
4
and
5
changing the exponents
\epsilon\in[0,\epsilon_{0}]
with the pair
(\epsilon,\mathcal{c})\in[0,\epsilon_{0}]\times[0,1]
.

Remark 6.10
.

In agreement with the above convention the projection
P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}
becomes
P^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
and the reduced Hamiltonian
\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}
becomes
\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
. The important new features appear in the formulation of Statement 2 in Theorem
2.18
and are due to the replacement of the family of functions
\widetilde{\text{\large{$\boldsymbol{\Psi}$}}}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}
in (
4.4
) by the family:

\widetilde{\text{\large{$\boldsymbol{\Psi}$}}}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\,:=\,\big\{\widetilde{\psi}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\alpha,p}:=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\alpha}\,\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\alpha}\,\psi^{\epsilon}_{p},\alpha\in\Gamma,\,p\in\underline{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}\big\}.

(6.11)

We shall sometimes use the notation

\psi^{\epsilon}_{\alpha,p}:=\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\alpha}\,\psi^{\epsilon}_{p}\,.

An important feature will be the presence of the objects
P^{\epsilon,0}_{\mathfrak{B}}
and
\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon,0}_{\mathfrak{B}}
referring to the projection and reduced Hamiltonian associated with the constant part of the perturbing magnetic field.

Related to (
6.11
) we shall also use the notations:

\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\gamma}\,:=\,\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\gamma}\,\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\gamma}\,,\qquad\forall\gamma\in\Gamma

where the
\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\gamma}
are the Zak translations defined in (
6.1
).

We repeat now the arguments and constructions in Subsection
4.2
, starting with an operator
\widetilde{Q}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
defined by the regular integral kernel (with rapid off-diagonal decay):

\mathfrak{K}[\widetilde{Q}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}](x,y):=\underset{(\alpha,p)\in\Gamma\times\underline{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}}{\sum}\,\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\alpha}\,\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\alpha}\,\psi^{\epsilon}_{p}(x)\,\overline{\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\alpha}\,\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\alpha}\,\psi^{\epsilon}_{p}(y)}.

We notice that:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\alpha}(x)\,\overline{\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\alpha}(y)}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}(x,\alpha)\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}(\alpha,y)=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}(x,y)\big[\exp\big(-i\int_{<x,\alpha,y>}B^{\epsilon}\big)\big]

(6.12)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}(x,y)\Big[1\,-\,i\epsilon\mathcal{c}\,\Big(\int_{<x,\alpha,y>}B^{\epsilon}\Big)\Big(\int_{0}^{1}ds\,\exp\big(-is\epsilon\mathcal{c}\int_{<x,\alpha,y>}B^{\epsilon}\big)\Big)\Big],

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{<x,\alpha,y>}B^{\epsilon}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\,C<x-\alpha><y-\alpha>,\qquad\forall\epsilon\in[0,\epsilon_{0}]\,,

and conclude that
[\widetilde{Q}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}]^{2}=\widetilde{Q}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\,+\,\mathscr{O}(\epsilon\mathcal{c})
(as bounded operators on
L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
). Thus, for some
\epsilon_{0}>0
and any
(\mathcal{c},\epsilon)\in[0,1]\times[0,\epsilon_{0}]

\sigma(\widetilde{Q}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}})\subset(-\mathcal{c}\epsilon,\mathcal{c}\epsilon)\bigcup(1-\mathcal{c}\epsilon,1+\mathcal{c}\epsilon)\,,

and we may define the following objects similar to those in Subsection
4.2
:

\begin{split}&P^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\,:=\,-(2\pi i)^{-1}\oint_{\mathscr{C}}d\mathcal{z}\,\big(\widetilde{Q}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}-\mathcal{z}{\rm{1}\hskip-3.0pt\mathbf{l}}\big)^{-1},\\
&\Theta^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\,:=\,-(2\pi i)^{-1}\oint_{\mathscr{C}}d\mathcal{z}\,\sqrt{\mathcal{z}^{-1}}\,\big(\widetilde{Q}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}-\mathcal{z}{\rm{1}\hskip-3.0pt\mathbf{l}}\big)^{-1},\end{split}

so that we have the following formula:

\begin{split}\mathfrak{K}[P^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}](x,y)&=\hskip-8.5359pt\underset{(\alpha,p)\in\Gamma\times\underline{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}}{\sum}\hskip-8.5359pt\Theta^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\big[\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\alpha}\,\big(\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\alpha}\,\psi^{\epsilon}_{p}\big)\big](x)\,\overline{\Theta^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\big[\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\alpha}\,\big(\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\alpha}\,\psi^{\epsilon}_{p}\big)\big](y)}\,.\end{split}

Finally we obtain the following Parseval frame of the Hilbert subspace
P^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\,L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
:

\text{\large{$\boldsymbol{\Psi}$}}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}:=\big\{\psi^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\alpha,p}:=\Theta^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\big[\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\alpha}\,\big(\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\alpha}\,\psi^{\epsilon}_{p}\big)\big],\ \forall(\alpha,p)\in\Gamma\times\underline{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}\big\}\,,

(6.13)

and given any bounded linear operator
T\in\mathbb{B}\big(P^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\,L^{2}(\mathcal{X})\big)
one may define its
magnetic
\mathfrak{B}
-matrix
as the matrix
\mathring{\mathfrak{M}}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}[T]\in\mathscr{M}_{\Gamma}[\mathscr{M}_{n_{\mathfrak{B}}}]
given by:

\big[\mathfrak{M}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}[T]_{\alpha,\beta}\big]_{p,q}\,:=\,\big(\psi^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\alpha,p}\,,\,T\,\psi^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\beta,q}\big)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}.

It is obvious from the above formulas (compared with the similar ones in Subsection
4.2
) that all the estimations obtained in Subsection
4.2
for the operators
\widetilde{Q}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}},P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}},\Theta^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}},\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}
remain true for
\widetilde{Q}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}},P^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}},\Theta^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}},\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
with the change of
\epsilon
into
\mathcal{c}\epsilon
. The important difference comes at the end of Subsection
4.4
, when, due to the presence of the functions
\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\alpha}\,\psi^{\epsilon}_{p}
in (
6.11
) that replace the functions
\tau_{-\gamma}\psi_{p}
in (
4.4
), Formula (
4.31
) becomes:

\displaystyle\big[\mathfrak{M}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}]_{\alpha,\beta}\big]_{p,q}=\big(\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\alpha}\psi_{p}\,,\,\big[\mathfrak{I}\mathit{nt}\,\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}\mathfrak{K}^{\circ}(h)\big]\mathfrak{T}^{\epsilon}_{\beta}\,\psi_{q}\big)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}=

(6.14)

\displaystyle\ =\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}(\alpha,\beta)\Big[\big[\mathring{\mathfrak{m}}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\widehat{H}]_{\alpha-\beta}\big]_{p,q}+\mathcal{c}\epsilon\big[\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}]_{\alpha,\beta}\big]_{p,q}\Big]\,,

where, for
\mathfrak{M}^{\epsilon,0}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon,0}_{\mathfrak{B}}]_{\alpha,\beta}=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}(\alpha,\beta)\mathring{\mathfrak{m}}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\widehat{H}]_{\alpha-\beta}
, we may use the general result in (
4.31
):

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}^{\epsilon,0}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\mathfrak{H}^{\epsilon,0}_{\mathfrak{B}}]_{\alpha,\beta}=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon}(\alpha,\beta)\big[\mathring{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathfrak{B}}[H_{\mathfrak{B}}]_{\alpha-\beta}+\epsilon\,\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\mathfrak{K}^{\circ}(h)]_{\alpha,\beta}\big]

(6.15)

with the particular choice
B=\epsilon{B}^{\bullet}
and also the estimation in Proposition
6.9
. Theorem
2.22
clearly follows from the above results.

From (
6.12
) we also deduce that:

\mathfrak{K}[\widetilde{Q}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{\mathfrak{B}}]=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}\,\big(\mathfrak{K}[P^{\epsilon,0}_{\mathfrak{B}}]\,+\,\epsilon\mathcal{c}\mathfrak{K}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{2}\big)\,,

where
P^{\epsilon,0}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is the same as
P^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}
in (
6.3
) in Proposition
6.3
and
\mathfrak{K}^{\epsilon,\mathcal{c}}_{2}
is a
BC^{\infty}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X})
integral kernel having rapid off-diagonal decay uniformly in
(\mathcal{c},\epsilon)\in[0,1]\times[0,\epsilon_{0}]
.

Appendix A
Brief reminder of the Bloch-Floquet Theory

On
L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
we consider the canonical unitary representation of
\mathbb{R}^{d}\cong\mathcal{X}
with the convention (used for the Weyl calculus)
\big(U(z)f\big)(x):=f(x-z)
.

We are interested in analyzing the structure of self-adjoint, lower-bounded operators of the form
T:\mathcal{D}(T)\rightarrow{L}^{2}(\mathcal{X})
(with
\mathcal{D}(T)
a dense linear subspace of
L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
) that have the property:

Hypothesis A.1
.

For any
\gamma\in\Gamma\cong\mathbb{Z}^{d}
the unitary
U(\gamma)
leaves
\mathcal{D}(T)
invariant and commutes with
T
.

More precisely, we shall be interested in operators satisfying Hypothesis
A.1
and being of the form
T=\overline{\mathfrak{Op}^{A^{\circ}}(F)}
as in Definition
2.7
. In
[
17
]
we have discussed the extension of the Bloch-Floquet theory from usual periodic differential operators, to this class of magnetic pseudo-differential operators and thus we shall very briefly recall the main results insisting a little bit more on the vector-bundle structure associated with the Bloch-Floquet decomposition.

Let us recall the Bloch-Floquet transformation:

\forall(\theta,x)\in\mathbb{T}_{*}\times\mathcal{X},\quad\big(\mathcal{U}_{BF}\varphi\big)(\theta,x):=\underset{\gamma\in\Gamma}{\sum}\,e^{-i<\mathfrak{s}_{*}(\theta),\gamma>}\,\varphi(x+\gamma),\quad\forall\varphi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{X}),

(A.1)

where the series is absolutely convergent due to the rapid decay of
\varphi\in\mathscr{S}(X)
. Noticing that for any
\varphi\in\mathscr{S}(X)
:

\big(\mathcal{U}_{BF}\varphi\big)(\theta,x+\alpha)=e^{i<\mathfrak{s}_{*}(\theta),\alpha>}\big(\mathcal{U}_{BF}\varphi\big)(\theta,x),\quad\forall(\theta,x)\in\mathbb{T}_{*}\times\mathcal{X},\ \forall\alpha\in\Gamma\,,

(A.2)

we define the linear space:

\forall\theta\in\mathbb{T}_{*}:\qquad\mathscr{F}_{\theta}:=\big\{F\in{L}^{2}_{\text{\tt loc}}(\mathcal{X}),\ \tau_{\alpha}F=\chi_{\theta}(-\alpha)F,\ \forall\alpha\in\Gamma\}\,,

(A.3)

where each element is completely determined by its restriction to the unit cell
\mathcal{E}\subset\mathcal{X}
and endow it with the scalar product:

\big(F,G\big)_{\theta}:=\big(F\big|_{\mathcal{E}},G\big|_{\mathcal{E}}\big)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{E})}.

(A.4)

Remark A.2
.

If we consider the family of smooth functions
\mathfrak{f}_{\xi}(x):=e^{i<\xi,x>}
indexed by
\xi\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
we notice that multiplication with
\mathfrak{f}_{\mathfrak{s}_{*}(\theta)}
defines a bijective linear map
\mathscr{F}_{\theta}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathscr{F}_{1}
where
\mathscr{F}_{1}
is clearly the space of square integrable
\Gamma
-periodic functions on
\mathcal{X}
.

Remark A.3
.

Given any function
f\in\mathscr{F}_{1}
we can associate with it the function
\mathring{f}:=f\circ\mathfrak{s}:\mathbb{T}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
and notice that it defines a unitary map
\mathscr{F}_{1}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
and also a bijection between smooth functions. Its inverse is clearly
\mathring{f}\mapsto\mathring{f}\circ\mathfrak{p}
.

It is rather evident that each
\mathscr{F}_{\theta}
with (
A.4
) is a Hilbert space and the two remarks above together with the constructions and arguments in
[
29
]
allow us to define the measurable vector fields
\big\{\mathfrak{f}_{\mathfrak{s}_{*}(\theta)}^{-1}\mathring{f},\ \mathring{f}\in{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T})\big\}_{\theta\in\mathbb{T}_{*}}
and the associated direct integral Hilbert space:

\mathscr{F}\,:=\,\int^{\oplus}_{\mathbb{T}_{*}}d\theta\,\mathscr{F}_{\theta}\,.

(A.5)

One can easily prove that the map
\mathcal{U}_{BF}
in (
A.1
) defines a unitary map
L^{2}(\mathcal{X})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathscr{F}
. An important result of the Bloch-Floquet theory is that, for any operator
T
satisfying Hypothesis
A.1
, its Bloch-Floquet (BF) representation
\mathcal{U}_{BF}T\mathcal{U}_{BF}^{-1}
is a decomposable operator on
\mathscr{F}
with respect to the direct integral structure (
A.5
), i.e.:

\mathcal{U}_{BF}T\mathcal{U}_{BF}^{-1}\,=\,\int^{\oplus}_{\mathbb{T}_{*}}d\theta\,\widehat{T}(\theta).

(A.6)

Let us also consider the Bloch-Floquet-Zak (BFZ) transformation defined as
{[\mathcal{U}_{BFZ}\varphi](\xi,x):=\mathfrak{f}_{-\xi}(x)[\mathcal{U}_{BF}\varphi](\mathfrak{p}_{*}(\xi),x)}
, or explicitely:

\forall(\xi,x)\in\mathcal{X}^{*}\times\mathcal{X},\quad\big(\mathcal{U}_{BFZ}\varphi\big)(\xi,x):=\underset{\gamma\in\Gamma}{\sum}\,e^{-i<\xi,x+\gamma>}\,\varphi(x+\gamma),\quad\forall\varphi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{X}).

(A.7)

One easily notice that
[\mathcal{U}_{BFZ}\varphi](\xi,x)=[\mathcal{U}_{BFZ}\varphi]\big(\xi,(\mathfrak{s}\circ\mathfrak{p})x\big)
and we can define:

\forall(\xi,\omega)\in\mathcal{X}^{*}\times\mathbb{T},\quad\big(\mathring{\mathcal{U}}_{BFZ}\varphi\big)(\xi,\omega):=[\mathcal{U}_{BFZ}\varphi]\big(\xi,\mathfrak{s}(\omega)\big),\varphi(x+\gamma),\quad\forall\varphi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{X}).

(A.8)

\mathscr{G}\,:=\,\big\{F\in{L}^{2}_{\text{\tt loc}}\big(\mathcal{X}^{*};L^{2}(\mathbb{T})\big),\ \tau_{\gamma^{*}}F=\mathfrak{f}_{-\gamma^{*}}F\big\}

(A.9)

and endow
\mathscr{G}
with the scalar product:

\big(F,G\big)_{\mathscr{G}}\,:=\,\int_{{\cal{B}}}d\hat{\xi}\,\big(F(\hat{\xi}),G(\hat{\xi})\big)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}.

(A.10)

Definition A.4
.

•

For any
p\in\mathbb{R}
we denote by:

–

\mathscr{F}^{p}_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{z}}^{*}}:=\mathscr{F}_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{z}}^{*}}\cap\mathscr{H}^{p}_{\text{\tt loc}}(\mathcal{X}),\quad\mathscr{F}^{p}:=\int^{\oplus}_{\mathbb{T}_{*}}d\theta\,\mathscr{F}^{p}_{\theta}
;

–

\mathscr{G}^{p}:=\big\{F\in{L}^{2}_{\text{\tt loc}}\big(\mathcal{X}^{*};\mathscr{H}^{p}(\mathbb{T})\big),\ \tau_{\gamma^{*}}F=\mathfrak{f}_{-\gamma^{*}}F\big\}
;

–

\mathscr{G}^{p}_{\infty}:=\big\{F\in{C}^{\infty}\big(\mathcal{X}^{*};\mathscr{H}^{p}(\mathbb{T})\big),\ \tau_{\gamma^{*}}F=\mathfrak{f}_{-\gamma^{*}}F\big\}
;

•

\mathscr{F}^{\infty}_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{z}}^{*}}:=\mathscr{F}_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{z}}^{*}}\cap{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{X})
,
\mathscr{F}^{\infty}:=\int^{\oplus}_{\mathbb{T}_{*}}d\theta\,\mathscr{F}^{\infty}_{\theta}
;

•

\mathscr{F}^{\infty}_{\infty}:=\big\{F\in{C}^{\infty}\big(\mathbb{T}_{*};C^{\infty}(\mathcal{X})\big),\ F(\theta,x+\gamma)=\chi_{\theta}(-\gamma)F(\theta,x),\ \forall\gamma\in\Gamma\big\}
;

•

\mathscr{G}^{\infty}:=\big\{F\in{L}^{2}_{\text{\tt loc}}\big(\mathcal{X}^{*};C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})\big),\ \tau_{\gamma^{*}}F=\mathfrak{f}_{-\gamma^{*}}F\big\}
;

•

\mathscr{G}^{\infty}_{\infty}:=\big\{F\in{C}^{\infty}\big(\mathcal{X}^{*};C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})\big),\ \tau_{\gamma^{*}}F=\mathfrak{f}_{-\gamma^{*}}F\big\}
.

If we denote by
\mathfrak{f}\in{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{X}^{*}\times\mathcal{X})
the function
\mathfrak{f}(\xi,x):=e^{i<\xi,x>}
we notice the evident equalities:

\mathscr{G}^{p}=\overline{\mathfrak{f}}\mathscr{F}^{p},\quad\mathscr{G}^{\infty}=\overline{\mathfrak{f}}\mathscr{F}^{\infty},\quad\mathscr{G}^{p}_{\infty}=\overline{\mathfrak{f}}\mathscr{F}^{p}_{\infty},\quad\quad\mathscr{G}^{\infty}_{\infty}=\overline{\mathfrak{f}}\mathscr{F}^{\infty}_{\infty}.

(A.11)

Using this framework, in Subsection 2.3 of
[
17
]
we extend a number of results in
[
48
,
46
]
from periodic differential operators to our class of pseudo-differential operators and recall them here in the following theorem:

Theorem A.5

(Bloch-Floquet decomposition)
.

If
H^{\circ}
is the magnetic pseudo-differential operator defined in Definition
2.7
, then it is a lower semi-bounded self-adjoint operator in
L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
with domain
\mathscr{H}^{p}(\mathcal{X})
(
p>0
), that commutes with all the translations
\big\{U_{\gamma},\,\gamma\in\Gamma\big\}
and satisfies the following properties:

1.

H^{\circ}
is unitarily equivalent with a direct integral (in the sense of
[
29
]
). More precisely, in the BF representation (
A.1
), we have the decomposition:

\mathcal{U}_{BF}H^{\circ}\mathcal{U}_{BF}^{-1}=\int^{\oplus}_{\mathbb{T}_{*}}\hskip-5.0ptd\theta\,\widehat{H^{\circ}}(\theta)

where each
\widehat{H^{\circ}}(\theta)
is a lower semi-bounded self-adjoint operator having as domain
\mathcal{D}(\widehat{H^{\circ}})=\mathscr{F}^{p}_{\theta}
.

2.

For any
\mathcal{z}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\sigma(H^{\circ})
, the map

\mathcal{X}_{*}\ni\xi\mapsto\widetilde{R}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{z}}(\xi):=\mathfrak{f}_{\xi}^{-1}\big(\widehat{H}^{\circ}\big(\mathfrak{p}_{*}(\xi)\big)-\mathcal{z}{\rm{1}\hskip-3.0pt\mathbf{l}}\big)^{-1}\mathfrak{f}_{\xi}\in\mathbb{B}\big(L^{2}(\mathbb{T})\big)

is well-defined and smooth.

3.

For each
\theta\in\mathbb{T}_{*}
and
\mathcal{z}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\sigma(H^{\circ})
, the resolvent
\widehat{R}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{z}}(\theta)
is a compact operator. Thus for any
\theta\in\mathbb{T}_{*}
, the spectrum of
\widehat{H^{\circ}}(\theta)
consists of discrete eigenvalues:

\sigma\big(\widehat{H^{\circ}}(\theta)\big)=\big\{\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}(\theta)\big\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{\bullet}}\,,

which satisfy
-\infty<\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}(\theta)<\widetilde{\lambda}_{k+1}(\theta)
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\bullet}
.

4.

If the geometric multiplicity of
\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}(\theta)
is
N_{k}(\theta)
, then we can re-index the spectrum by using ”simple” eigenvalues in the following way:

\widetilde{\lambda}_{1}(\theta)=\lambda_{1}(\theta)=\lambda_{2}(\theta)=\dots=\lambda_{N_{1}}(\theta)<\widetilde{\lambda}_{2}(\theta)=\lambda_{N_{1}+1}(\theta)=\dots=\lambda_{N_{1}+N_{2}}(\theta)<\dots

In this way, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\bullet}
, the function
\mathbb{T}_{*}\ni\theta\mapsto\lambda_{k}(\theta)\in\mathbb{R}
is continuous.

The Bloch eigenprojections.

In order to define the eigenprojections
\widehat{\pi}_{k}(\theta)
associated with the Bloch eigenvalues
\big\{\lambda_{k}(\theta)\big\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{\bullet}}
, we apply the following procedure. To each
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\bullet}
and
\theta\in\mathbb{T}^{d}_{*}
, we introduce the minimal labelling of
\lambda_{k}(\theta)
:

\nu(k,\theta)=\inf\big\{j\in\mathbb{N}_{\bullet}\,,\,\lambda_{j}(\theta*)=\lambda_{k}(\theta)\big\}\,,

and define the eigenprojections by the following formulas:

\begin{split}\widehat{\pi}_{k}(\theta):=&\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\mathscr{C}_{k}(\theta)}d\mathfrak{z}\,\big(\widehat{H^{\circ}}(\theta)-\mathfrak{z}{\rm{1}\hskip-3.0pt\mathbf{l}}\big)^{-1}\in{\mathbb{L}(\mathscr{F}_{\theta})}&\mbox{ if }\nu(k,\theta)=k\,,\\
=0&\mbox{ if }\nu(k,\theta)<k\,,\end{array}\right.\end{split}

(A.12)

where
\mathscr{C}_{k}(\theta)\subset\mathbb{C}
is a circle surrounding
\lambda_{k}(\theta)
in a anticlockwise direction and no other point from
\sigma\big(\widehat{H^{\circ}}(\theta)\big)
. They define measurable
\mathbb{B}(\mathscr{F}_{\theta})
-valued functions on
\mathbb{T}_{*}
. Some standard arguments using the ellipticity of
h\in S^{p}_{1}(\mathcal{X}^{*}\times\mathcal{X})
imply the following statement.

Theorem A.6
.

Under the hypothesis of Theorem
A.5
the range of any finite dimensional orthogonal projection
\widehat{\pi}_{k}(\theta)
is contained in the space of regular functions
\mathscr{F}_{\theta}^{\infty}
.

Appendix B
Some properties of the magnetic pseudo-differential calculus

B.1
Main definitions and properties

In
[
52
]
the authors prove that starting from the basic principle that the influence of magnetic fields on Hamiltonian systems may be described by the so-called
’minimal coupling procedure’
that consists in modifying the momentum variables by a translation with the magnetic vector potential, one may define a ’twisted’ Weyl quantization providing the algebra of quantum observables of the given Hamiltonian system in magnetic fields. The procedure is developed in
[
41
,
43
,
53
,
2
,
24
,
25
]
and we have developed it in connection with periodic Hamiltonians in magnetic fields in (
[
44
]
,
[
19
]
,
[
12
]
-
[
17
]
.)

Definition B.1
.

Given a regular magnetic field
B\in{{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}_{\text{\tt bd}}^{2}(\mathcal{X})
with vector potential
A\in{{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}_{\text{\tt pol}}^{1}(\mathcal{X})
such that
B=dA
, we have the following explicit formula for the magnetic quantization for any
\Phi\in\mathscr{S}(\Xi)
:

\begin{split}&\big(\mathfrak{Op}^{A}(\Phi)\phi\big)(x)\,=\,\int_{\mathcal{X}}dy\int_{\mathcal{X}^{*}}d\eta\,e^{-i\int_{[x,y]}A}\,e^{i<\eta,x-y>}\,\Phi\big((x+y)/2,\eta\big)\,\phi(y),\\
&\hskip 284.52756pt\forall\phi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{X}),\,\forall x\in\mathcal{X}.\end{split}

(B.1)

Remark B.2
.

The explicit formula of
\mathfrak{Op}^{A}(\Phi)\in\mathcal{L}\big(\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{X});\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{X})\big)
in Definition
B.1
allows us to obtain a simple formula for the integral kernel
\mathfrak{K}^{A}[\Phi]\in\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X})
of this operator:

\mathfrak{K}^{A}[\Phi]\,=\,\Lambda^{A}\,\big[\big(({\rm{1}\hskip-3.0pt\mathbf{l}}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}^{*}})\Phi\big)\circ\Upsilon\big].

(B.2)

where
\Upsilon:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\ni(x,y)\mapsto\big((x+y)/2,x-y\big)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
is a linear bijection with Jacobian 1.

Remark B.3
.

It is proven in
[
52
]
that for a vector potential
A
with components of class
C^{\infty}_{\text{\tt pol}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
the map
\mathfrak{Op}^{A}:\mathscr{S}(\Xi)\rightarrow\mathcal{L}\big(\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{X});\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{X})\big)
defines a linear and topological isomorphism
\mathfrak{Op}^{A}:\mathscr{S}(\Xi)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{L}\big(\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathcal{X});\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{X})\big)
that has a canonical extension to a linear and topological isomorphism
\mathfrak{Op}^{A}:\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\Xi)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{L}\big(\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{X});\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathcal{X})\big)
.

Definition B.4
.

Due to the Remark
B.3
, for any magnetic field with components of class
BC^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we can define the following bilinear map
\sharp^{B}:\mathscr{S}(\Xi)\times\mathscr{S}(\Xi)\rightarrow\mathscr{S}(\Xi)
given by the equality:
\mathfrak{Op}^{A}(\Phi)\mathfrak{Op}^{A}(\Psi):=\mathfrak{Op}^{A}(\Phi\sharp^{B}\Psi)
and called
the magnetic Moyal product
.

Remark B.5
.

The results in
[
52
]
imply that the magnetic Moyal product is a bilinear jointly continuous map that has the following explicit formula:

\big(\Phi\sharp^{B}\Psi\big)(X)\,=\,4^{-d}\int_{\Xi}dZ\int_{\Xi}dZ^{\prime}\,e^{-2i(<\xi-\zeta,x-z^{\prime}>-<\xi-\zeta^{\prime},x-z>)}\,e^{-i\Theta^{B}(x;z,z^{\prime})}\,\Phi(Z)\,\Psi(Z^{\prime})

(B.3)

where
\Theta^{B}(x;z,z^{\prime})
is the flux of the
2
-form
B
through the following triangle in
\mathcal{X}
:

\mathcal{T}_{x;z,z^{\prime}}:=\big\{P_{x;z,z^{\prime}}(s,s^{\prime}):=z+z^{\prime}-x+2s(x-z)+2s^{\prime}(z-z^{\prime})\,,\,s\in[0,1],\,s^{\prime}\in[0,s]\big\}.

(B.4)

We shall constantly use the following properties of the magnetic pseudodifferential calculus, considering a magnetic field
B
with components of class
BC^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and a choice of a vector potential
A
with components of class
C^{\infty}_{\text{\tt pol}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
:

Theorem B.6

(Theorem 2.1 in
[
43
]
.)
.

The Moyal product extends to a bilinear, continuous map
(see also Theorem 2.6 in
[
41
]
)

S^{p_{1}}_{\rho}(\mathcal{X}^{*},\mathcal{X})\times S^{p_{2}}_{\rho}(\mathcal{X}^{*},\mathcal{X})\ni(F,G)\,\mapsto\,F\sharp^{B}G\in S^{p_{1}+p_{2}}_{\rho}(\mathcal{X}^{*},\mathcal{X}).

Proposition B.7

(Proposition 4.23 in
[
52
]
.)
.

The operator
\mathfrak{Op}^{A}:\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\Xi)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{L}\big(\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{X});\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathcal{X})\big)
restricts to a linear continuous map
\mathfrak{Op}^{A}:S^{p}_{\rho}(\mathcal{X}^{*},\mathcal{X})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}\big(\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{X});\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{X})\big)\bigcap\mathcal{L}\big(\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathcal{X});\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathcal{X})\big)
for any
(p,\rho)\in\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]
.

Theorem B.8

(Theorem 2.6 in
[
43
]
and Theorem 3.1 in
[
41
]
.)
.

If
f\in S^{0}_{\rho}(\mathcal{X}^{*},\mathcal{X})
, with any
\rho\in[0,1]
, then
\mathfrak{Op}^{A}(f)\in\mathbb{B}\big(L^{2}(\mathcal{X})\big)
.

The next theorem is proved in
[
43
]
(Theorem 2.6) (see also Theorem 3.1 in
[
41
]
):

Theorem B.9
.

Given
\rho\in(0,1)
, a magnetic field
B\in{{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}_{\text{\tt bd}}^{2}(\mathcal{X})
and an associated vector potential of class
{{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}_{\text{\tt pol}}^{1}(\mathcal{X})
, if
f\in S^{0}_{\rho}(\Xi)
, then
\mathfrak{Op}^{A}(f)\in\mathbb{B}\big(L^{2}(\mathcal{X})\big)
.

We also need the following results proven in
[
43
]
(Theorem 2.7 and Proposition 2.4):

Theorem B.10
.

For
F\in S^{p}_{1}(\Xi)
positive and elliptic, with
p>0
, the operator
\mathfrak{Op}^{A}(F):\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
is essentially self-adjoint and its closure has the domain:

\mathscr{H}^{p}(\mathcal{X})\,:=\,\big\{f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{X})\,,\,\mathfrak{Op}(\mathfrak{m}_{p})f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{X})\big\}.

Moreover, for any
\mathcal{z}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\sigma\big(\overline{\mathfrak{Op}^{A}(F)}\big)
there exists
\mathfrak{r}^{B}_{\mathcal{z}}\in S^{-p}_{1}(\Xi)
such that:

\big(\overline{\mathfrak{Op}^{A}(F)}-\mathcal{z}{\rm{1}\hskip-3.0pt\mathbf{l}}\big)^{-1}=\mathfrak{Op}^{A}(\mathfrak{r}^{B}_{\mathcal{z}}).

(B.5)

Using Propositions 1.3.3 and 1.3.6 in
[
1
]
and Lemma A.4 in
[
53
]
, one obtains easily the following result:

Proposition B.11
.

Suppose we have a magnetic field
B\in{{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}_{\text{\tt bd}}^{2}(\mathcal{X})
with a vector potential
A\in{{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}_{\text{\tt pol}}^{1}(\mathcal{X})
.

1.

If
F\in S^{p}_{1}(\Xi)
for any
(p,\rho)\in\mathbb{R}\times\{0,1\}
, then
\mathfrak{K}^{A}[F]\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X})
as given in (
B.2
) is a smooth function on
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\setminus\big\{(x,x)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X},\,x\in\mathcal{X}\big\}
having rapid decay in the variable
x-y\in\mathcal{X}
.

2.

If
F\in S^{p}_{1}(\Xi)
with
p<0
, then
\mathfrak{K}^{A}[F]\in L^{1}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X})
.

3.

F\in S^{-\infty}(\Xi)
if and only if
\mathfrak{K}^{A}[F]\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X})
with rapid decay in the directions orthogonal to the diagonal of
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
, together with all its derivatives.

B.2
Some estimations in weak regular magnetic fields

Let us formulate a technical result which - in our current setting - replaces Proposition 3.5 in
[
19
]
and which can be proved with similar arguments.

Proposition B.12
.

Given
(m,s)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
, there exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
such that for any
\epsilon\in[0,\epsilon_{0}]
there exists a continuous bilinear map

S^{m}_{1}(\Xi)_{\Gamma}\times S^{s}_{1}(\Xi)_{\Gamma}\ni(F,G)\mapsto\mathcal{r}_{\epsilon}\big(F,G)\in S^{m+s-2}_{1}(\Xi)

such that for
B
as in (
2.10
) and and
B^{\circ}
as in Hypothesis (
2.5
) and using notation (
2.11
):

F\sharp^{\epsilon}G\,=\,F\sharp^{\circ}G\,+\,\epsilon\,\mathcal{r}_{\epsilon}\big(F,G)

Moreover, the bilinear map
\mathcal{r}_{\epsilon}
is uniformly bounded with respect to
\epsilon\in[0,\epsilon_{0}]
.

Proposition B.13
.

Suppose given
p\in\mathbb{R}
and a real symbol
F\in{S}^{p}_{1}(\Xi)
such that
\overline{\mathfrak{Op}^{\epsilon}(F)}
is a self-adjoint operator in
L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
for any
\epsilon\in[0,\epsilon_{1}]
with some
\epsilon_{1}>0
.
For
K\subset\big[\mathbb{C}\setminus\sigma\big(\overline{\mathfrak{Op}^{\circ}(F)}\big)\big]
with
B^{\circ}=dA^{\circ}
as in Hypothesis
2.5
, there exists
\epsilon_{0}\in(0,\epsilon_{1}]
such that:

1.

K\subset\big[\mathbb{C}\setminus\sigma\big(\overline{\mathfrak{Op}^{\epsilon}(F)}\big)\big]
for any
\epsilon\in[0,\epsilon_{0}]
.

2.

We consider the magnetic symbols defined by
\mathfrak{Op}^{\epsilon}\big(\mathfrak{r}^{\epsilon}_{F,\mathcal{z}}\big)=\big(\overline{\mathfrak{Op}^{\circ}(F)}-\mathcal{z}{\rm{1}\hskip-3.0pt\mathbf{l}}\big)^{-1}
; then
\mathfrak{r}^{\epsilon}_{F,\mathcal{z}}\in{S}^{-p}_{1}(\Xi)
for
p\geq 0
and
\mathfrak{r}^{\epsilon}_{F,\mathcal{z}}-1\in{S}^{p}_{1}(\Xi)
for
p<0
and the map
K\ni\mathcal{z}\mapsto\mathfrak{r}^{\epsilon}_{F,\mathcal{z}}\in S^{p}_{1}(\Xi)
, is continuous for the Fréchet topology on
S^{p}_{1}(\Xi)
, uniformly in
(\epsilon,\mathcal{z})\in[0,\epsilon_{0}]\times{K}
.

3.

We have the equality:

\mathfrak{r}^{\epsilon}_{F,\mathcal{z}}\,=\,(1+\epsilon\,\mathcal{r}_{\epsilon}(F,\mathfrak{r}^{\circ}_{F,\mathcal{z}})\big)^{-}_{\epsilon}\,{\sharp^{\epsilon}}\,\mathfrak{r}^{\circ}_{F,\mathcal{z}}.

Proof.

We shall only present the small changes to be done in the proof of Proposition 1.8 in
[
19
]
. In fact, the main point is to replace Equation (3.22) in
[
19
]
by the equality:

1=\big(F-\mathcal{z}\big)\sharp^{\circ}\mathfrak{r}^{\circ}_{F,\mathcal{z}}=\big(F-\mathcal{z}\big)\sharp^{\epsilon}\mathfrak{r}^{\circ}_{F,\mathcal{z}}\,-\,\epsilon\,\mathcal{r}_{\epsilon}\big(F,\mathfrak{r}^{\circ}_{F,\mathcal{z}}\big)

with
\mathcal{r}_{\epsilon}\big(F,\mathfrak{r}^{\circ}_{F,\mathcal{z}}\big)
in
S^{-2}_{1}(\Xi)
uniformly for
\epsilon\in[0,\epsilon_{0}]
. Once we made these replacements in
[
19
]
the arguments in the proof of Proposition 1.8 of
[
19
]
remain true.
∎

as in (

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}[\mathcal{I}^{\epsilon}_{1}(\phi,\psi)]_{\alpha,\beta}:=\int_{\mathcal{X}}dx\int_{\mathcal{X}}dy\ \widetilde{\Omega}^{\epsilon}(x,\alpha,y)\,\overline{\phi(x-\alpha)}\,\psi(y-\beta)\,,\\
{[\mathcal{I}^{\epsilon}_{2}(\phi,\psi)]_{\alpha,\beta}}:=\int_{\mathcal{X}}dx\int_{\mathcal{X}}dy\ \widetilde{\Omega}^{\epsilon}(\alpha,x,\beta)\,\overline{\phi(x-\alpha)}\,\psi(y-\beta)\,,\end{array}\right.\ \forall(\phi,\psi)\in[\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{X})]^{2}\,,

(B.6)

taking into account that:

\displaystyle\int_{<\alpha,x,y>}\hskip-11.38092ptB^{\epsilon}\,

(B.10)

Finally, we obtain the following formulas:

\displaystyle[\mathcal{I}^{\epsilon}_{1}(\phi,\psi)]_{\alpha,\beta}:

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{X}}dx\int_{\mathcal{X}}dy\ \widetilde{\Omega}^{\epsilon}(x,\alpha,y)\,\overline{\phi(x-\alpha)}\,\psi(y-\beta)

\displaystyle\quad+i\epsilon\int_{\mathcal{X}}dx\int_{\mathcal{X}}dy\hskip-5.69046pt\underset{1\leq j<k\leq d}{\sum}\hskip-5.69046pt\Phi_{\alpha}[B^{\epsilon}]_{j,k}(x,y)\,\big((x-\alpha)_{j}\overline{\phi(x-\alpha)}\big)\,\big((y-\beta)_{k}\psi(y-\beta)\big)

\displaystyle\big[\mathfrak{m}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{B}}[\mathfrak{K}[h^{\circ}]]_{\alpha,\beta}\big]_{p,q}=

(B.14)

B.3.3

\displaystyle\big(\partial^{c}_{v}\partial_{z}^{a}\widetilde{\Omega}^{\epsilon}\big)(z+v/2,\gamma,z-v/2)=\partial^{c}_{v}\partial_{z}^{a}\Big[\exp\Big(-i\epsilon\int_{<z+v/2,\gamma,z-v/2>}B^{\epsilon}\Big)\Big].

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{p}\exp\big(-i\epsilon{F}(t)\big)=

(B.17)

\displaystyle\quad=\underset{1\leq N\leq p}{\sum}\,(-i\epsilon)^{N}\underset{p_{1}+\cdots+p_{N}=p}{\sum}\,C_{p}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{N})\,\underset{1\leq\ell\leq N}{\prod}\big[\big(\partial_{t}^{p_{\ell}}F\big)(t)\big]\,\Big[\exp\big(-i\epsilon{F}(t)\big)\Big]\,,

as the flux of the 2-form

\displaystyle\int_{<z+v/2,\gamma,z-v/2>}\hskip-42.67912ptB^{\epsilon}\hskip 22.76228pt

(B.20)

Appendix C
Some basic properties of frames on Hilbert spaces

In this appendix,
{\cal H}
denotes a complex Hilbert space.

Definition C.1
.

1.

We call frame of
{\cal H}
, an at most countable family of vectors
\big\{\psi_{p}\,,\,p\in\mathbb{N}_{\bullet}\big\}\subset{\cal H}
such that there exists two positive constants
0<A\leq B
satisfying:

A\big\|f\|_{{\cal H}}^{2}\,\leq\,\underset{M\nearrow\infty}{\lim}\underset{1\leq p\leq M}{\sum}\big|\big(\psi_{p}\,,\,f\big)_{{\cal H}}\big|^{2}\,\leq\,B\big\|f\|_{{\cal H}}^{2},\quad\forall f\in{\cal H}.

(C.1)

2.

A frame of
{\cal H}
is called a Parseval frame, when the following equality is verified for any
f\in{\cal H}
:

\big\|f\big\|^{2}_{{\cal H}}=\underset{p\in\mathbb{N}_{\bullet}}{\sum}\,\big|\big(\psi_{p}\,,\,f\big)_{{\cal H}}\big|^{2}\,,

(C.2)

with the series converging in
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N}_{\bullet})
.

3.

Given a frame
\vec{\psi}\big\{\psi_{p}\,,\,p\in\mathbb{N}_{\bullet}\big\}\subset{\cal H}
, we introduce its coordinate map
\mathfrak{C}
as:

{\cal H}\ni f\mapsto\big(\mathfrak{C}_{\vec{\psi}}f):=\{\big(\psi_{p}\,,\,f\big)_{{\cal H}}\big\}_{p\in\mathbb{N}_{\bullet}}\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N}_{\bullet}).

(C.3)

Lemma C.2
.

Given a Parseval frame in
{\cal H}
, the following identities are valid, for
(f,g)\in{\cal H}\times{\cal H}
:

\big(f\,,\,g\big)_{{\cal H}}=\underset{p\in\mathbb{N}_{\bullet}}{\sum}\,\big(f,\psi_{p}\big)_{{\cal H}}\big(\psi_{p},g\big)_{{\cal H}},\qquad f=\underset{p\in\mathbb{N}_{\bullet}}{\sum}\,\big(\psi_{p}\,,\,f\big)_{{\cal H}}\,\psi_{p}\,,

(C.4)

with the series converging in
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N}_{\bullet})
and resp. in the strong topology of
{\cal H}
and the coordinate map
\mathfrak{C}_{\vec{\psi}}:{\cal H}\rightarrow\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N}_{\bullet})
is an isometry that may not be surjective.

Proof.

Let us write the polarization identity in
{\cal H}
:

\big(f\,,\,g\big)_{{\cal H}}=(1/4)\underset{0\leq k\leq 3}{\sum}i^{k}\big\|f+(-i)^{k}g\big\|^{2}_{{\cal H}}\,,

and use (
C.2
) to obtain:

\displaystyle\big(f\,,\,g\big)_{{\cal H}}

\displaystyle=(1/4)\underset{0\leq k\leq 3}{\sum}i^{k}\big\|f+(-i)^{k}g\big\|^{2}_{{\cal H}}=(1/4)\underset{0\leq k\leq 3}{\sum}i^{k}\underset{p\in\mathbb{N}_{\bullet}}{\sum}\,\big|\big(\psi_{p}\,,\,f+(-i)^{k}g\big)_{{\cal H}}\big|^{2}

\displaystyle=\underset{p\in\mathbb{N}_{\bullet}}{\sum}\,(2/4)\Big(2\Re\big(\psi_{p}\,,\,f\big)_{{\cal H}}\overline{\big(\psi_{p}\,,\,g\big)}_{{\cal H}}-2\Im\big(\psi_{p}\,,\,f\big)_{{\cal H}}\overline{\big(\psi_{p}\,,\,g\big)}_{{\cal H}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\underset{p\in\mathbb{N}_{\bullet}}{\sum}\big(\psi_{p}\,,\,g\big)_{{\cal H}}\overline{\big(\psi_{p}\,,\,f\big)}_{{\cal H}}.

For the second identity one proves using the first identity that
\sum\limits_{p=1}^{M}\,\big(\psi_{p}\,,\,f\big)_{{\cal H}}\psi_{p}
is a sequence converging weakly to
f
and being Cauchy for the strong topology, due to the Parseval condition.
∎

Proposition C.3
.

If given a pair of vectors
(v,w)\in{\cal H}\times{\cal H}
we denote by
|v><w|
the linear rank 1 operator
|v><w|u:=(w,u)_{{\cal H}}v
, the following limit exists in the weak operator topology:

\underset{p\nearrow\infty}{\lim}|\psi_{p}><\psi_{p}|\,=\,{\rm{1}\hskip-3.0pt\mathbf{l}}.

We also recall the following theorem in
[
9
]
(Theorem 5.5.1.)

Theorem C.4
.

A sequence
\vec{f}\equiv\{f_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
in
{\cal H}
is a frame for
{\cal H}
if and only if the application:

\vec{c}\equiv\{c_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}\mapsto\mathfrak{S}_{\vec{f}}\big(\vec{c}\big)\,:=\,\underset{k\in\mathbb{N}_{\bullet}}{\sum}c_{\ell}\,f_{\ell}

is a well defined surjective map from
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N}_{\bullet})
onto
{\cal H}
.

Remark C.5
.

If we denote by
\{\mathcal{e}_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
the canonical orthonormal basis of
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N}_{\bullet})
and use the notation introduced in the beginning of Subsection
2.2
we have the identities:

\mathfrak{C}_{\vec{\psi}}=\underset{p\in\mathbb{N}_{\bullet}}{\sum}\mathcal{e}_{\mathfrak{p}}\bowtie\psi_{p},\qquad\text{I}\hskip-2.0pt\text{I{\sf d}}_{{\cal H}}=\mathfrak{C}_{\vec{\psi}}^{*}\mathfrak{C}_{\vec{\psi}},\qquad\mathfrak{C}_{\vec{\psi}}\mathfrak{C}_{\vec{\psi}}^{*}=:\mathfrak{P}_{\vec{\psi}}

with
\mathfrak{P}_{\vec{\psi}}\in\mathbb{B}\big(\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N}_{\bullet})\big)
the orthogonal projection on the range of
\mathfrak{C}_{\vec{\psi}}
.

Let us consider the map:

\mathbb{B}\big({\cal H}\big)\ni\,T\,\mapsto\,\mathfrak{W}_{\vec{\psi}}[T]:=\mathfrak{C}_{\vec{\psi}}\,T\,\mathfrak{C}_{\vec{\psi}}^{*}\in\mathbb{B}\big(\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N}_{\bullet})\big).

(C.5)

We can associate with
\mathfrak{W}_{\vec{\psi}}[T]
the infinite matrix of the operator
\mathfrak{C}_{\vec{\psi}}\,T\,\mathfrak{C}_{\vec{\psi}}^{*}\in\mathbb{B}\big(\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N}_{\bullet})\big)
with respect to the canonical orthonormal basis
\{\mathcal{e}_{p}\}_{p\in\mathbb{N}_{\bullet}}
.

Proposition C.6
.

The map
\mathfrak{W}_{\vec{\psi}}:\mathbb{B}\big({\cal H}\big)\,\rightarrow\,\mathbb{B}\big(\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N}_{\bullet})\big)
in (
C.5
) is an injective
C^{*}
-algebras morphism.
